# The Profound Mysteries of the Grotto-Heaven 
<note>original: "洞天奧旨" (Dòngtiān Àozhǐ). In Chinese tradition, "Grotto-Heavens" are sacred sites of spiritual revelation. In a medical context, this title suggests the text unveils the deep, hidden secrets of healing, specifically focusing on "external medicine"—the treatment of skin conditions, sores, and surgical issues.</note>

**The Profound Mysteries of the Grotto-Heaven**, **Medical Science** <note>original: "醫學" (Yīxué). This identifies the work's classification as a medical text.</note>

<summary>This is the title page for a significant Chinese medical manuscript known for its specialized focus on surgery and the treatment of external ailments.</summary>
<keywords>Dongtian Aozhi, Traditional Chinese Medicine, surgery, external medicine, Qing Dynasty medical texts</keywords>

# The Profound Mysteries of the Grotto-Heaven, Volume 6

->Written by **Chen Shiduo** (style name Jingzhi, sobriquet Yuangong) of Shanyin; Assisted by his grandson **Zhi**<-

->Compiled by **Tao Shiyu** (style name Shangbai, sobriquet Cunji) of Kuaiji — Proofread by **Fang Bo** (style name Huishi) of Jiaxing<-

## Abscesses Occurring Above the Breast

**Abscesses above the breast** <note>original: "胸乳上發" (xiōng rǔ shàng fā). "Fa" refers to the eruption or outbreak of a sore or abscess.</note> occur at acupuncture points such as <term>Huozhong</term> (Chest Center), <term>Shenzang</term> (Spirit Storehouse), and <term>Lingxu</term> (Spirit Ruins). These points all belong to the **Foot Shaoyin Kidney Meridian**. <note>The Kidney Meridian is one of the twelve primary pathways of energy (qi) in the body; although the kidneys are in the lower body, this pathway travels up the chest.</note> The symptoms of this condition often include a feeling of the heart being suspended as if one is hungry, yet having no desire to eat, accompanied by a dry tongue and a swollen throat.

This is caused by **Heat in the Heart** that is unable to descend and communicate with the **Kidneys**. <note>In traditional theory, the "Fire" of the Heart and the "Water" of the Kidneys must balance each other. If this communication fails, internal heat can build up.</note> This failure leads to the energy (*qi*) of the Kidney Meridian being suppressed and forced outward, which results in the growth of <term>abscesses and sores</term> <note>original: "癰瘍" (yōngyáng). These are broad terms for inflammatory swellings, carbuncles, or ulcers.</note> located on the upper portion of the chest or breast. These may appear on the left side, or the right side; in severe cases, they may even break out on both sides simultaneously. All...

<meta>Center fold (banxin) text: Abscesses Above the Breast</meta>
<meta>Marginal text at bottom left: Dazhun Hall (The Publishing House)</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the pathology of abscesses occurring above the breast, attributing them to a lack of communication between Heart-Fire and Kidney-Water, which manifests along the Kidney Meridian.</summary>
<keywords>Chen Shiduo, Kidney Meridian, Heart-Kidney communication, breast abscesses, acupuncture points, Huozhong, Shenzang, Lingxu</keywords>

--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of abscesses on the upper chest and breast, explaining the underlying disharmony between the Heart and Kidney systems and providing specific medicinal formulas for treatment.</meta>

When **Kidney-Water** is unable to aid and balance **Heart-Fire**, it is essential to significantly supplement the Water and assist it with medicine that disperses the Heart-Fire. In doing so, the Water will be replenished, and the fire-toxin will easily dissipate. If this is not treated promptly, the toxin will attack the Heart, and one's chances of survival will become remote. <note>In traditional theory, the Heart (Fire) and Kidneys (Water) must remain in constant communication. If the "Water" of the Kidney is too weak to cool the "Fire" of the Heart, the resulting heat becomes a "toxin" that manifests physically as an abscess.</note>

### Ten-Isle Powder <gloss>A formula transmitted by the Immortal True Lord Wu to treat abscesses occurring on the chest and breast</gloss>

<note>The "Ten Isles" refer to the mythical lands of the immortals in Daoist lore. True Lord Wu likely refers to Wu Taizi, a legendary figure in Chinese medicine and Daoism.</note>

*   **Ginseng** <gloss>2 mace (approx. 7.5g)</gloss>
*   **Processed Rehmannia Root** <gloss>2 taels (approx. 75g)</gloss> <note>A heavy dose of this herb is used here to "supplement the Water" or Kidney essence.</note>
*   **Cornelian Cherry** <gloss>3 mace (approx. 11g)</gloss>
*   **Raw Licorice Root** <gloss>2 mace (approx. 7.5g)</gloss>
*   **Polygala Root** <gloss>2 mace (approx. 7.5g)</gloss> <note>Known as *Yuanzhi*, this herb is used to calm the mind and open the channels of the Heart.</note>
*   **Dwarf Lilyturf Tuber** <gloss>1 tael (approx. 37.5g)</gloss>
*   **Honeysuckle Flower** <gloss>1 tael (approx. 37.5g)</gloss> <note>A primary herb for clearing heat and resolving toxins in skin infections.</note>
*   **Poria with Hostwood** <gloss>3 mace (approx. 11g)</gloss> <note>Called *Fushen*, this is a fungus that grows around pine roots; it is used specifically to soothe the Heart-spirit.</note>
*   **Coptis Rhizome** <gloss>1 mace (approx. 3.7g)</gloss>
*   **Dandelion** <gloss>4 mace (approx. 15g)</gloss>

Decoct in five bowls of water until eight-tenths of the liquid remains. Take several doses in succession.

->The ailment will spontaneously subside<-

### Tangerine Seed Powder <gloss>To treat "Inward-blowing" and "Outward-blowing" breast abscesses in women</gloss>

<note>original: "吹乳" (chuī rǔ). This is a traditional term for acute mastitis or breast abscesses. "Outward-blowing" usually refers to those occurring during lactation, while "inward-blowing" refers to those occurring during pregnancy or regardless of nursing.</note>

Mandarin orange seeds: one seed for every year of the patient’s age <gloss>dried between "Yin and Yang" tiles until charred and brittle, then ground into a fine powder</gloss>. <note>The "Yin and Yang" tiling method involves placing medicinal ingredients between two ceramic roof tiles to roast them evenly without direct contact with flame.</note> Take with warm aged wine, then cover with a blanket to induce sweating; the patient will recover.

### Chest Abscess

A chest abscess <term>Xiongfa; an eruption on the chest</term> occurs at four specific acupuncture points: **Jade Hall**, **Chest Center**, **Central Courtyard**, and **Turtledove Tail**. <note>These points—Yutang (CV18), Shanzhong (CV17), Zhongting (CV16), and Jiuwei (CV15)—are located along the midline of the sternum, directly over the heart and solar plexus.</note> There is also an abscess that develops on the chest called a **"Well Abscess"** <term>Jingju</term>. When this condition first begins, it looks like a small bean and the color of the skin does not change. It must be treated early.

If it is not treated promptly, the toxins will sink downward into the abdomen, which inevitably leads to death. These four points belong to the network of the **Conception Vessel** <term>Ren Mai; the primary meridian running up the front midline of the body</term>. These points are located on the outer perimeter of the Heart.

In all cases, "evil" <note>In traditional medicine, "evil" (xie) refers to pathogenic factors like heat, wind, or toxins that invade the body.</note> must not be allowed to encroach upon the Heart; once the Heart is invaded, death follows immediately. If a pathogen from the internal organs were to invade the Heart, could there be any path for escape? <note>The original text uses "path to the palm," a metaphor for an easy or accessible route, suggesting that if the pathogen moves, it moves decisively.</note> Given that these four points are in such close proximity to the "Heart Sovereign" <note>Traditional Chinese medicine views the Heart as the "monarch" or "sovereign" (Jun) of the body's organs.</note>, how could one remain carefree and unconcerned? Naturally, the toxin enters the internal organ directly, as the path is both convenient and swift. Even though it is said that these meridians belong specifically to the Conception Vessel, yet the Conception Vessel...

<summary>This page describes "Chest Abscesses" (Xiongfa) and "Well Abscesses" (Jingju), warning that their proximity to the Heart makes them exceptionally lethal if not treated immediately. A remedy using aged mandarin orange seeds is provided as an initial treatment to induce sweating.</summary>

<keywords>Chest Abscess (Xiongfa), Well Abscess (Jingju), Mandarin Orange Seeds, Conception Vessel (Ren Mai), Heart Sovereign, Acupuncture points, Jade Hall (Yutang), Chest Center (Shanzhong)</keywords>

Page Eleven

# Volume 6: The Hall of Great Refinement

This vessel <note>Referring to the "Conception Vessel" or *Ren Mai* described on the previous page.</note> is known as the **Sea of Yin Vessels**. It flows through all the Yin channels of the body, circulating in an endless loop. Once an **abscess toxin** <term>Yongdu; a virulent, localized infection</term> is present, the Yin energy can no longer flow. Furthermore, even before the abscess manifests, it is the lack of flow in the Yin vessel that allows **fire toxins** <note>In traditional medicine, "fire toxins" refer to severe inflammatory pathogens characterized by heat, redness, and rapid spread.</note> to gather and stagnate rather than disperse, eventually forming the abscess. Since the abscess has already formed, how can one expect the energy to circulate through the Yin channels without further obstruction? Naturally, the stagnation becomes more severe, and the blockages grow tighter. If these toxins are not quickly scattered—as if dispersing a cloud of stars—then the fire toxin will "return to the Heart," resulting in death in an instant.

<note>Wu Peng is a legendary figure in Chinese medicine, often regarded as one of the ancient "divine physicians" or shamans of the prehistoric era.</note>

Vaccaria seed <gloss>three mace</gloss>
Boil in water.

Should the area have already ulcerated and broken, more doses must be taken before a cure is achieved.

### Forehead Eruptions

**Forehead Eruption** <term>E-fa; a carbuncle or inflammatory swelling on the forehead</term> refers to an outbreak occurring at the **Gathered Bamboo** point <note>original: *Cuanzhu* (BL-2); an acupuncture point located in the indentation at the inner end of the eyebrow</note> on the forehead. Now, although the **Crooked Curve** <note>original: *Qucha* (BL-4)</note> and Gathered Bamboo points belong to the **Great Yang Channel** <term>Taiyang; specifically the Bladder meridian</term>, they are located near the **Governing Vessel** <term>Du Mai; the "Sea of Yang" channel that runs up the spine and over the midline of the head</term>. This area is also a site where **Yin and Yang** energies meet and converge.

At the onset of this condition, there will inevitably be a headache, an aversion to both cold and heat, a neck that feels as stiff as a wooden board, and a waist that feels as if it were breaking. These are the clear, defining symptoms of a Great Yang disorder. However, the Great Yang **Bladder** and the **Lesser Yin Kidney Channel** <note>original: *Shaoyin*</note> are paired as "exterior" and "interior" organs. When treating the "fire" of the Great Yang, one must also nourish the "water" of the Lesser Yin. In doing so, the Bladder will not become parched. With this internal moisture, the body can easily produce a sweat. Once the sweat is released, the fire-toxins will follow it and be completely dispersed through the skin's surface. 

Otherwise, if one's sole focus is on "pushing to the surface" <note>Inducing sweating to expel a pathogen</note>, and if the patient is a person suffering from **Yin deficiency** <term>a lack of the body's cooling and moistening fluids</term>, then it is forbidden to further induce...

<summary>This page concludes a treatment for chest abscesses and introduces "Forehead Eruptions," explaining how infections at the eyebrow points relate to the Great Yang and Governing channels. The author argues that treating such head infections requires balancing the "fire" of the Bladder channel with the "water" of the Kidney channel to safely induce healing sweat.</summary>

<keywords>Forehead Eruption, E-fa, Gathered Bamboo point, Cuanzhu, Great Yang, Taiyang, Governing Vessel, Du Mai, fire-toxin, Yin deficiency, Bladder channel, Kidney channel</keywords>

I fear that because of the sweating, the patient will become even more **deficient** <note>A state of depletion in the body's vital energy (Qi) or nourishing fluids</note>, and conversely, the **fire-toxin** <note>A traditional term for a virulent, heat-based infection</note> will refuse to dissipate quickly.

## Dual Outbreaks on Both Flanks

A **Flank Outbreak** <term>Xie-fa; an inflammatory swelling or carbuncle occurring on the sides of the torso</term> refers to an eruption occurring at the **Qimen** <note>Liver-14; "Gate of Hope," located near the nipple line in the 6th intercostal space</note> and **Zhangmen** <note>Liver-13; "Completion Gate," located at the tip of the 11th rib</note> acupuncture points. In antiquity, this was named *Chuilu* <note>Literally "Blowing and Dripping"; a historical name for abscesses occurring in the rib or breast area</note>, and it was said to be a woman's ailment. ### Volume VI: The Outbreak of Carbuncles and Abscesses — Great Elegance Hall

Indeed, both men and women may suffer from this; the condition may erupt on either the left or the right side. This region corresponds to the **Foot *Jueyin* Liver Meridian** <term>One of the twelve primary energy pathways in the body, which governs the flow of Qi and influences the ribs and flanks</term>.

However, there are also instances where the swelling reaches upward to the **渊液 (*Yuanye*)** <note>Acupoint Gallbladder 22, located on the side of the chest below the armpit</note> and **辄筋 (*Zhejin*)** <note>Acupoint Gallbladder 23, located near the ribs</note> points. These two points belong to the **Foot *Shaoyang* Gallbladder Meridian** <term>The meridian paired with the Liver; it runs along the side of the body</term>. Since the Liver and Gallbladder share an **interior-exterior relationship** <term>Biao-Li; a concept where two organs are functionally and energetically linked as a pair</term>, a disease of the Liver will inevitably affect the Gallbladder. Therefore, one must not neglect the Liver while attempting to treat the Gallbladder.

In the Gallbladder, there is typically an abundance of **Qi** <note>Vital energy</note> and a scarcity of **Blood**, whereas the Liver possesses an abundance of **Blood** and a scarcity of **Qi**. In general, it is appropriate to **supplement both Qi and Blood**; one must never recklessly use "hot" medicinal agents <note>Medicines with a heating property used to expel cold, which can be dangerous if the condition is caused by "fire-toxin" or inflammation</note>.

Most people in this world experience much anger and irritation. Once there is frustration or suppression of one's will, the **Qi** is immediately stirred, causing a sense of distention and fullness in both flanks, which consequently forms an abscess. Those abscesses that grow on the outside of the skin are the milder cases. There are also those that grow *inside* the flanks. Ancient writers did not discuss internal flank abscesses, and many people today do not believe they exist. Yet, who knows that flank pain so severe that the area cannot even be touched by the hand indicates that an abscess is growing on the **lobes of the liver**?

Abscesses of the liver are formed half from anger and half from melancholy. Those resulting from **melancholy** <note>original: 憂鬱 (yōuyù)</note> develop slowly, while those resulting from **anger** <note>original: 氣惱 (qìnǎo)</note> develop suddenly. Melancholy and anger...

<summary>This page explains the anatomical and energetic causes of flank abscesses, linking them to the Liver and Gallbladder meridians and emotional triggers like anger and melancholy. It highlights the existence of dangerous internal liver abscesses that are painful to the touch.</summary>
<keywords>Liver Meridian, Gallbladder Meridian, Qi and Blood, Liver Abscess, emotional etiology, internal medicine, acupoints</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the medical discourse on flank disorders, shifting from the physiological relationship between the Liver and Gallbladder to the specific pathology of internal carbuncles caused by emotional exhaustion and the depletion of "Liver-Blood."</meta>

These emotions <note>Referring to the anger and irritation mentioned at the end of the previous page</note> are all capable of scorching and drying out the **Liver-Blood** <term>In traditional Chinese medicine, the Liver is said to 'store' the blood; this blood is necessary to moisten the organ and keep its energy flowing smoothly</term>. Once the Liver-Blood has been dried, the Liver becomes extremely parched. Without blood to nourish the Liver, and with the cycle of worry and irritation continuing without end, how could one possibly avoid the formation of carbuncles, the knots of suppressed anger, and the swelling that results in an abscess? 

However, because these carbuncles grow internally, they cannot be seen. Yet, there are external signs: the skin outside the flank will surely manifest a reddish-purple color. The pain will also definitely be on the left side <note>In traditional diagnosis, the left flank is primarily associated with the Liver meridian</note>, and the tongue will certainly appear bluish-purple in hue. In this world, there are those who suffer from flank pain for only a few days and then suddenly die; this is precisely because the toxins from these carbuncles have caused internal collapse. 

The method of treatment focuses primarily on **Calming the Liver** <term>Ping Gan; a therapeutic strategy to soothe the Liver's rising energy and restore emotional and physical balance</term>, assisted by medicinal ingredients that **Drain Fire** <term>Xie Huo; using bitter or cold herbs to clear inflammatory heat</term> and remove toxins. One must never delay or hesitate until the abscess has already ruptured internally before attempting a rescue, as that will almost always result in failure.

There are also cases where a deep-seated ulcer <note>original: 疽 *ju*, a more serious, deeper-rooted abscess than a carbuncle</note> grows beneath the flanks, located between the **Jingmen** <note>Capital Gate; Acupoint Gallbladder 25, located on the side of the waist</note> and **Daimai** <note>Girdle Vessel; Acupoint Gallbladder 26</note> acupoints. The pain and itching are so intense they reach the heart, feeling like the sharp prick of a needle. If the ulceration gradually spreads as far as the navel, the patient will die. At the first sign of swelling, the waist and flanks cannot be rotated, and there is pain in all the joints of the bones—a pain that connects directly to the heart. This is also known as a **Heart-Transmitting Deep-Seated Ulcer**. The method of treatment for this is the same as that used for flank carbuncles.

<summary>This page explains how chronic anger scorches the Liver-Blood, leading to dangerous internal carbuncles and "Heart-Transmitting" ulcers that can be fatal if not treated by calming the Liver and draining fire before they rupture.</summary>
<keywords>Liver-Blood, Internal Carbuncle (Yong), Deep-seated Ulcer (Ju), Flank Pain, Jingmen point, Daimai point, Heart-Transmitting Ulcer, Calming the Liver</keywords>

# Flowing Injection Eruptions

Flowing Injection Eruptions <term>Liuzhu Fa; a type of multiple subcutaneous abscesses or systemic infections that appear to "flow" or migrate from one area of the body to another</term> are what are known as "Mother-and-Child" eruptions. They first appear on the back, then stream and spread to the four limbs, coming and going without a fixed location. These types of sores and ulcers <term>Chuang Yang; a general term for various skin swellings and infections</term> are mostly "Yang" conditions <note>In traditional medicine, "Yang" conditions are typically acute, red, hot, and painful, as opposed to chronic, cold "Yin" conditions</note>, generally caused by Wind-Heat...

# Neck-Encircling Eruptions

**Neck-Encircling Eruptions** are those that manifest on the neck. They wrap around the throat and the back of the neck so completely that not a single patch of healthy skin remains. In severe cases, the area becomes swollen, massive, and turns a deep reddish-black. It is colloquially known as the **"Head-Falling Abscess"** <note>original: 落頭癰 (Luo Tou Yong); so named because the swelling and decay are so severe they threaten the integrity of the neck</note> and is the condition referred to as the **"Heavenly Carbuncle"** in the *Spiritual Pivot* <note>The *Lingshu*, an ancient foundation text of Chinese medicine</note>.

One must urgently purge the "fire" <note>toxic heat</note> from this condition. The head and neck are the domains of the acupuncture points for the **Hand and Foot Lesser Yang** <term>Shaoyang; energetic channels associated with the Triple Burner and Gallbladder systems</term> as well as the **Foot Bright Yang** <term>Yangming; the channel associated with the Stomach</term>. If the fire within these three channels is not quickly drained, the heat becomes trapped between the neck and the throat. When this heat cannot be rapidly dissipated, the toxic heat will inevitably travel downward to the **Abyssal Fluid** <term>Yuanye; an acupuncture point on the side of the chest, indicating here that the toxin is sinking deeper into the body's cavity</term>, moving from the exterior to the interior.

Once the toxin enters the interior, it first damages the **Conception and Governing Vessels** <term>Ren and Du; the two primary "seas" of yin and yang energy in the body</term> and internally "smokes" the **Lungs and Liver**. The patient will experience bouts of fever and chills, physical exhaustion, mental confusion and agitation, feelings of terror, and an inability to eat. There are cases where the patient dies in as little as ten days. How can one not be cautious? Therefore, it is imperative to treat this condition as early as possible.

The skin and flesh at this site are quite different from other parts of the body. In other places, the skin and flesh grow either horizontally or vertically, and in both cases, it is easy for the muscle and skin to regenerate. However, at the site of a **Hanging Carbuncle** <term>Xuan Yong; a painful, deep-seated abscess located in the perineum</term>, there are vertical fibers within the horizontal flesh and horizontal lines within the vertical fibers. It is the most difficult kind of wound to close. 

Once there is damage to this area, it is not easy to achieve a successful recovery. Although the development of a carbuncle here is attributed to the presence of "damp-heat" <note>A traditional Chinese medical concept where environmental or dietary factors create a heavy, inflammatory condition in the body</note>, they are all ultimately formed because of a craving for sex and a fondness for wine. Now, a craving for wine and sex is common among men; if one can moderate their drinking and practice sexual abstinence, why should it be impossible to achieve a recovery? 

What people do not realize is that the **scrotum** <term>Shen nang; literally "Kidney Sac"</term> is the meeting point of the **Thoroughfare and Conception vessels** <term>Chong and Ren Mai; two vital energy channels that regulate the body's life force and reproductive essence</term>, and it is also the place where various "tendons" <note>original: 筋 (jin); in this context, it refers to the complex network of tendons, ligaments, and nerves in the pelvic floor</note> gather. Because the sac is hollow and empty, it is very easy for Qi and Blood to stagnate and gather there; thus, it swells easily and grows large, which is why it is so difficult to heal. 

In severe cases, the tissue often rots and sloughs away. There are cases where the rot progresses until only the **testicles** <term>Gaowan</term> remain, and there are even instances where everything rots and falls off, yet the patient does not die. This is due to the excellence of medicinal regulation and care. The formula used, the **Divinely Miraculous Pellet for Expelling Evil** <term>Zhu xie zhi shen dan</term>, is most extraordinary; it can be used whether the abscess has already burst or has not yet ruptured. If one uses the **Eight Immortals Pellet** <term>Ba xian dan</term>, although it is also miraculous, it can only be used for a **Scrotal Abscess** <term>Nang yong</term> that has not yet burst.

# Abscesses Erupting Opposite the Navel

"Opposite-Navel Eruptions" are those that break out on the lower back at the <term>Gate of Life acupuncture point</term><note>original: 命門 (Mìngmén); the Mingmen or "Gate of Life" is located on the spine, directly behind the navel, and is considered the source of the body's fundamental energy.</note>. The Gate of Life point is situated directly opposite the navel.

The Gate of Life is the master of the twelve meridians. If the master is not clear and healthy, then the twelve officials—the internal organs—are in grave danger. Furthermore, when <term>carbuncles and phlegmons</term><note>original: 癰疽 (Yōngjū); these are deep-seated, painful, and often life-threatening skin infections or abscesses.</note> develop there, the interior of the Gate of Life becomes filled with nothing but <term>evil fire</term><note>original: 邪火 (Xiéhuǒ); pathogenic heat or inflammation that disrupts the body's balance.</note>. In such a state, how can there be any spark of life remaining?

It is often misunderstood that the Gate of Life originally stores <term>True Fire</term><note>original: 眞火 (Zhēnhuǒ); the vital, life-sustaining warmth and Yang energy of the body.</note>. It is only after this True Fire has weakened that the toxins of "evil fire" are able to linger within.

However, True Fire prefers to reside deep within the acupuncture point, whereas evil fire prefers to erupt outside the point. When an abscess grows on the exterior of the Gate of Life, it is a sign of the evil fire moving outward. If treated with the proper methods, this actually offers a chance for survival; it is not as deadly as toxic growths occurring at the <term>Kidney Shu point</term><note>original: 腎俞 (Shènshù); an acupuncture point located near the Gate of Life, but associated more directly with the structural health of the kidneys.</note>.

Nevertheless, even though the evil fire has manifested on the outside, if the True Fire is not nourished with <term>water</term><note>original: 水 (Shuǐ); referring to the cooling, nourishing Yin fluids of the body.</note>, the "Righteous Fire" of the body will become increasingly deficient. If this happens, the evil fire that had moved to the exterior may sink back into the interior. Moreover, when evil fire is blazing intensely, one must use "water" to subdue it. Unless one greatly replenishes the <term>True Water</term><note>original: 眞水 (Zhēnshuǐ); the essential Yin fluids or Kidney essence.</note>, the evil fire will not dissipate. If the evil fire does not dissipate, the toxins will be difficult to eliminate. Furthermore, since the Gate of Life point is...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of abscesses located at the lower spine. While the previous page focused on the "Gate of Life" (lower back), this section moves lower to the "Tail Gate" (coccyx) and introduces the pathology of the "Sharp Carbuncle." The Kidneys, which dwell in the "Palace of Water" among the internal organs, are Yin in nature and govern the bones. The corresponding Yang bowel (the Bladder) possesses Greater Yang energy <note>original: *Taiyang* (太陽)</note> that governs the exterior surface of the body.

The development of a boil in this specific region <note>The sacrum/coccyx.</note> is triggered by "fire toxin" from the Greater Yang Bladder channel manifesting externally. However, the root cause is the depletion of the Lesser Yin <note>original: *Shaoyin* (少陰)</note> Kidney-Water energy internally; because the Water is exhausted, it cannot restrain the Fire from within. When the Fire is left unchecked, the path of the Governing Vessel <note>original: *Du Mai* (督脈), the primary energy channel running up the spine</note> becomes parched and dry. Consequently, fire flares up at the "Tail Gate" <note>original: *Weilu* (尾閭), the coccyx/sacrum area</note>, and because the Water cannot rise from that point to provide irrigation to the rest of the path, a "Sharp Carbuncle" <note>original: *Rui Ju* (銳疽)</note> is born. 

The term "Sharp" refers to the extreme ferocity and swiftness of the fire toxin. This type of abscess is the most difficult to endure; it is hard to produce pus, which is a clear physical proof of the total lack of "Water" or moisture in the body. The proper treatment is to profoundly replenish the Kidney Water while adding "Interior-Supporting" medicines <term>Tuoli (托裡): A medical strategy using tonics to bolster the body's energy so it can push toxins outward from the deep tissues.</term>. One should add a small amount of Frankincense and Myrrh to help expel pus and alleviate pain; in this way, a recovery may be hoped for. 

As for emotional agitation and sexual desire, these must be strictly and absolutely avoided. If one violates these prohibitions even once, the condition will, at best, result in a chronic fistula; at worst, it will lead to death. How can one not be cautious?

### Hand-back Carbuncles <note>Appended with Hand-palm Carbuncles</note>

Hand-back carbuncles <note>original: *Shoubei Fa* (手背發), a term for a diffuse, aggressive phlegmon or inflammation on the back of the hand.</note> erupt at the two acupuncture points known as the "Central Islet" <note>original: *Zhongzhu* (中渚), TE3</note> and the "Fluid Gate" <note>original: *Yemen* (液門), TE2</note>. These two points are part of the Hand Lesser Yang Triple Burner meridian. 

While the Triple Burner has no physical organ-shape <note>In Traditional Chinese Medicine, the Triple Burner or *Sanjiao* is a functional energy system rather than a single anatomical organ like the stomach.</note>, its meridians and pathways are very much tangible and have form. This meridian begins at the "Surging Pass" <note>original: *Guanchong* (關衝), TE1</note>; the points "Central Islet" and "Fluid Gate" are the second and third points following the "Surging Pass." 

Since the Triple Burner lacks a physical internal organ, its meridian path itself serves as its "residence" or internal office. To develop a deep-seated ulcer <term>Ju (疽): Unlike a superficial boil, a *Ju* ulcer is deep, flat, and hard, often indicating a more serious internal depletion.</term> in this specific location is considered to be "near the residence" <note>Meaning the infection has struck the primary location where this organ system's energy dwells.</note>. Therefore, the condition is also extremely serious. 

Furthermore, the Hand Lesser Yang system is a "residence" characterized by having abundant Qi but sparse Blood. Because there is insufficient blood to facilitate the transformation of the infection into pus, these sores often take a dangerous turn: what began as an active "Yang" condition often transforms into a sunken "Yin" state <note>This indicates the body's inability to push the toxin out, leading to a chronic, non-healing, and potentially necrotic wound.</note>.

Use "Release Karmic Debt Decoction" or "Pistil Pearl Decoction" in heavy doses. If the ulcer has not yet burst, it will naturally dissipate; if it has already burst, it will promote the growth of new flesh and heal the wound.

It is also argued that the **Stomach Meridian** <term>Stomach Meridian: In Traditional Chinese Medicine, the Foot Yangming Stomach Meridian is considered unique for being "abundant in both Qi and Blood," making it highly resilient.</term> is a storehouse rich in both **Qi** and **Blood**. An **abscess** <note>original: *yong* (癰), a large, swelling sore</note> occurring on the Stomach Meridian might therefore seem to be a minor matter. However, the points **Chongyang**, **Xiangu**, and **Neiting** <note>These are acupuncture points (ST42, ST43, and ST44) located on the top of the foot.</note> are the very beginnings of where the **Foot Yangming Meridian** <note>The Stomach Meridian</note> emerges and converges. At these locations, the Qi and Blood have not yet reached their full strength.

Furthermore, because these points are situated at the lower extremity of the foot and have not yet ascended to the body’s "Main Storehouse" <note>The torso or the Stomach organ itself</note>, despite the general abundance of Qi and Blood in the meridian as a whole, one cannot say that the Qi and Blood within these specific points are plentiful. Therefore, when a **deep-seated ulcer** <note>original: *ju* (疽), a more dangerous, bone-deep infection than a standard abscess</note> arises here, one must not rely solely on the theory of "abundant Qi and Blood" to recklessly employ medicines that **detoxify** or **aggressively attack fire** <note>"Fire" refers to inflammatory heat; aggressive "fire-attacking" herbs can be harsh and depleting to a patient's energy.</note>.

At the onset of the condition, it causes the patient to experience fever, nausea, pain, itching, and numbness. All such cases should be treated according to the principles discussed previously. Generally, success is achieved by performing the work of **dispersion** <note>Moving the stagnation and heat out of the area</note> from within a foundation of **supplementation** <note>Strengthening the patient’s underlying constitution</note>.

It is also said that the "back of the foot" refers to the **instep**. An ulcer occurring on the **heel** is also known as a **"Rabbit’s Bite."** This area pertains to the **Foot Taiyang** <note>The Bladder Meridian</note> and the **Shenmai** point <note>Point BL62</note>, which is the source where the **Yin and Yang Motility Vessels** <note>Two of the Eight Extraordinary Vessels that regulate movement and the opening/closing of the eyes</note> originate. These conditions all arise from **accumulated heat** within the **internal organs** <note>original: *zangfu* (臟腑), the yin and yang organ systems</note>.

Furthermore, a toxic eruption in the **center of the sole** is called a **"Shuttle-threading Furuncle."** <note>original: *chuansuo ding* (穿梭疔). A "clove" or furuncle is a small, hard, deep-rooted and very painful sore; "shuttle-threading" describes its piercing nature.</note> This is caused by **Kidney deficiency**, and thus **tonifying the Kidney** <note>Strengthening the Kidney's essence and energy</note> is of primary importance in its treatment.

# Pulmonary Abscess and Pulmonary Atrophy

A **pulmonary abscess** <term>Pulmonary Abscess: Known as *feiyong* (肺癰) in Chinese medicine, this refers to a severe infection or abscess within the lung tissue, typically presenting with fever and the eventual coughing up of foul-smelling pus.</term> is an abscess that grows within the lobes of the lungs. At its onset, there will certainly be pain in the chest and diaphragm. During bouts of coughing, the pain becomes even more extreme.

If the area is pressed by the hand, the shortness of breath <note>original: *qiji* (氣急), meaning rapid or labored breathing</note> increases even further. The pulse at this time is tight and rapid; this indicates that the pulmonary abscess is about to rupture.

Within the throat, the patient will first notice a fishy and foul odor. This is followed by the vomiting of pus and blood. Once this occurs, the pulse is merely rapid but no longer tight; this indicates that the pulmonary abscess has already ruptured. Generally, those that have not yet ruptured are easy to dissipate, while those that have already ruptured are difficult to treat. However, if one treats the condition with the correct method...

In terms of treatment, how can one abandon the method of **supplementing the lungs** <note>original: *bufei* (補肺)</note> to seek another way?

However, the lung is a **delicate organ** <term>Delicate Organ: Known as *jiaozang* (嬌臟) in Chinese medicine, this suggests the lungs are sensitive to environmental changes and do not tolerate harsh, heavy, or overly cooling medicinal substances well.</term> that does not easily tolerate medicine or food. One cannot supplement the lung qi directly; however, if one supplements the qi of the **Stomach-Earth**, then the **Lung-Metal** qi will naturally flourish <note>This follows the "Five Phases" theory where Earth (the Stomach and Spleen) is the "mother" of Metal (the Lungs). This is the superior method for treatment before the abscess has ruptured and released pus. Furthermore, there is a condition where, after a long-lasting cough, the bronchial tubes are damaged. Lung atrophy, however, arises from **taxation injury** <term>Taxation Injury: Known as *laoshang* (勞傷), this refers to illness caused by physical overwork, emotional stress, or chronic internal exhaustion that depletes the body's fundamental reserves.</term>, and treatment should be gradual and gentle.

For fire-toxins, one should supplement the "center" <note>Refers to the Spleen and Stomach, the root of digestion.</note> while using drainage techniques. For taxation injury, one should supplement the center while incorporating clearing <note>Clearing refers to mild cooling and hydrating actions to soothe the lungs.</note> agents. Therefore, when treating a pulmonary abscess, one should use large doses, but for lung atrophy, one should use small, frequent doses. These belong to the **Conception Vessel** <note>original: *Renmai* (任脈), the "Sea of Yin" meridian that runs down the center of the front of the body.</note> and are not the same as a condition originating inside the lungs. While **yang patterns** <note>Acute, red, and swollen inflammatory conditions that are usually easier to resolve.</note> are easy to treat, **yin patterns** <note>Deep-seated, chronic, or "sunken" conditions that lack heat and energy, making them more dangerous.</note> can be fatal. # Volume Seven: Large Intestine Abscesses

The swelling associated with these **abscesses** <term>abscess (yong): in traditional Chinese surgery, this refers to a localized, painful swelling resulting from the stagnation of "heat-toxins" and blood, often involving pus formation.</term> occurs in both the large and small intestines alike. How, then, do I differentiate between them? One who bends their right leg has a **large intestine abscess** <note>original: *dachang yong* (大腸癰); in modern clinical practice, this often corresponds to appendicitis.</note>, whereas one who bends their left leg has a **small intestine abscess** <note>original: *xiaochang yong* (小腸癰).</note>.

The world commonly says that a large intestine abscess is easy to treat, while a small intestine abscess is difficult to heal. However, the **fire** <note>In this context, "fire" refers to acute inflammation and pathological heat.</note> of a large intestine abscess can be purged by clearing it out through the **dregs** <gloss>solid waste or feces</gloss>, while the fire of a small intestine abscess can be purged by draining it through the **urine** <gloss>liquid waste</gloss>.

Nevertheless, there are cases of large or small intestine abscesses where the patient does not bend their leg. This is because those that form **inside the intestine** <note>intraluminal or within the intestinal wall</note> necessarily cause the leg to bend, while those that form **outside the intestine** <note>extraluminal or within the peritoneal cavity</note> do not result in a bent leg. 

If the pain is on the left side and fixed in the left-center without moving, it is a small intestine abscess. If the pain is on the right and remains fixed on the right, it is a large intestine abscess. Differentiating the symptoms in this manner is absolutely infallible. 

One must note: for an abscess forming inside the intestine, one may allow it to **rupture** <gloss>to burst or ulcerate</gloss> naturally. However, for an abscess forming outside the intestine, one must absolutely never allow it to rupture. Because there is no exit path for an abscess outside the intestine, once it ruptures and releases **pus** <gloss>purulent matter</gloss>, where will that pus go? If these toxins remain trapped within the abdominal cavity, there is no case that does not end in death. Therefore, the method of treatment must—

<summary>This page describes the differential diagnosis between large and small intestine abscesses using leg positioning and pain location, while warning of the fatal danger of an external abscess rupturing into the abdominal cavity.</summary>
<keywords>Large Intestine Abscess, Small Intestine Abscess, differential diagnosis, appendicitis, internal vs external abscess, peritonitis, abdominal pain</keywords>

...merely.

One must seek treatment urgently; it is absolutely vital that one does not follow old habits and neglect treatment until the abscess ruptures and bursts before starting care, as this will result in the loss of the patient’s life.

**Honeysuckle** <gloss>three ounces</gloss>

->**Abscesses and Ulcers**<-

<page-num>Page Nine</page-num>

The upper part of the **buttocks** belongs to the domain of the **Foot Taiyang Bladder Meridian** <note>The Bladder Meridian (*Zu Taiyang Pangguang Jing*) is the longest channel in the body, running from the eyes, over the head, down the back, and into the feet.</note>. This meridian is characterized by having an abundance of **Blood** but a scarcity of **Qi** <note>In Traditional Chinese Medicine (TCM), meridians are categorized by the relative amounts of *Qi* (vital energy) and Blood they carry; Taiyang is "Many Blood, Little Qi."</note>. Because of this, its Qi finds it particularly difficult to circulate thoroughly to this specific area. 

Therefore, while one might not develop an **abscess** <term>yong; a painful, swollen, and pus-filled inflammation of the skin and underlying tissues</term> here easily, once one does form, the flesh will inevitably suffer great pain. This occurs because the lack of Qi prevents proper movement and circulation. Thus, at the very onset of the condition, one should immediately use medicinal ingredients that **supplement Qi** <note>original: *buqi*; strengthening the body's vital energy to provide the "push" needed to expel pathogens.</note> in order to **generate Blood**, while assisting them with substances that **transform toxins** and **remove Fire** <note>original: *huadu quhuo*; "Fire" refers to the pathological heat causing the redness, heat, and swelling of the infection.</note>. In this way, the abscess will easily disperse. 

However, if one fails to supplement the Qi and instead focuses exclusively on attacking the Fire-toxin, the Qi will become even more deficient and the Blood will be further consumed. The Fire-toxin will then become difficult to remove, and the internal flesh will become hollow, making it difficult for the wound to close and heal.

If the abscess is located slightly toward the **hip bone** <note>original: *kuagu*; the pelvic or ilium area.</note>, near the **Huantiao** <note>Acupoint Gallbladder 30; located on the lateral side of the hip, crucial for treating sciatica and hip pain.</note> and **Chengfu** <note>Acupoint Bladder 36; located at the midpoint of the transverse crease of the buttock.</note> acupoints, then it belongs to the region of the **Foot Shaoyang** <note>The Gallbladder Meridian (*Zu Shaoyang Dan Jing*).</note>. The Foot Shaoyang is a "treasury" characterized by **scant Blood and abundant Qi**, which appears to be the opposite of the Foot Taiyang. Nevertheless, when using "attacking" methods within a "supplementing" framework, the treatment for these two meridians is identical: simultaneously supplementing both Qi and Blood while assisting with the transformation of toxins and the removal of Fire. By doing so, one will never fail to achieve miraculous results.

<summary>This page explains the meridian theory behind treating abscesses on the buttocks and hips, emphasizing that despite differences in Qi and Blood levels between the Bladder and Gallbladder meridians, both require a balance of strengthening the body and clearing toxins.</summary>
<keywords>Bladder Meridian, Gallbladder Meridian, Qi and Blood, carbuncles, Huantiao acupoint, Chengfu acupoint, abscess treatment, Tai Wei Tang</keywords>

<header>Esoteric Meanings of the Grotto Heavens: Volume Seven</header>

### Bone Abscess
<note>original: *Guyong*; in traditional Chinese medicine, this refers to a deep-seated, chronic inflammatory condition affecting the bone, similar to what modern medicine calls osteomyelitis. It often involves the formation of a "sequestrum"—a piece of dead bone that separates from the healthy bone.</note>

After an abscess forms, the wound refuses to close. Amidst the rotting and decaying flesh, a piece of bone suddenly grows and protrudes, causing agonizing and unbearable pain. Common folk refer to this as a "Multiple Bone Abscess." Yet, who would suspect that this is actually a transformation caused by the toxins of Damp-Heat?
<note>"Damp-Heat" (*Shire*) is a diagnostic concept in Chinese medicine where internal "dampness" (stagnant fluids or metabolic waste) combines with "heat" (inflammation or infection) to create a persistent, toxic condition.</note>

Now, these bone-protruding abscesses can appear anywhere on the body; they are not limited to the area of the *Changqiang* acupoint.
<note>original: *Changqiang*; also known as "Long Strong" or Governing Vessel 1, located at the tip of the tailbone. The author is clarifying that while bone infections often occur near the spine or pelvis, they can manifest in any limb or joint.</note>

The condition initially arises from the overconsumption of fruit and foods that are "raw and cold" in nature. It eventually develops due to procrastination and a failure to seek timely treatment. This allow the internal dampness to pool and stagnate, which in turn generates heat. When this heat reaches an extreme level, it "solidifies" or transforms into bone. There is often one...
<note>The Chinese medical view suggests that "cold" foods damage the digestive system (Spleen), leading to dampness. If this dampness lingers and becomes infected, it "cooks" the local fluids and minerals into the hard, bone-like protrusions seen in chronic wounds.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the pathology of Bone Abscesses (osteomyelitis), explaining that they are caused by internal Damp-Heat resulting from poor diet and neglected treatment rather than just localized injury.</summary>
<keywords>Bone Abscess, Chen Shiduo, Damp-Heat, Changqiang acupoint, External Medicine, osteomyelitis, diet and health</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Bone Abscesses" from the start of Volume Seven, moving from the general definition of the condition to its chronic symptoms and the philosophy of treatment.</meta>

### The Progression of the Disease

The condition may persist for two years without healing. Often, a piece of bone will fall out from the wound, or perhaps a tiny splinter, or the bone may appear to have holes eaten into it by decay. <note>original: *zhushi zhi yan*; literally "eyes of moth-eaten corrosion," referring to the pitted appearance of a bone sequestrum in chronic osteomyelitis.</note> Every three to five months, another piece of bone will slough off. Even if one uses <term>iron forceps<gloss>original: *jiatie*; surgical tweezers or small tongs used to extract debris from a wound</gloss></term> to remove the fragment, the opening of the wound still refuses to grow new flesh, and after a short time, more bone is produced. The patient cries out in agony from morning until night, hoping for recovery, yet the end of their suffering remains nowhere in sight.

### Internal vs. External Treatment

What is the reason for this? It is because practitioners only understand external treatments and do not understand internal treatments. While these bone fragments are difficult to dissolve with external applications, they are easily dissolved through internal medicine. This is because the "multiple bones" found in these <term>scrofulous abscesses<gloss>original: *luoju*; a term for chronic, deep-seated sores that often affect the lymph nodes or bones</gloss></term> are actually formed from formless pathogens. It is not that there is "true bone" existing within the flesh; rather, it is something that resembles bone but is not actually bone. <note>Chen Shiduo is making a brilliant observation here: he distinguishes between the patient's skeletal structure ("true bone") and the pathological, bone-like "sequestra" produced by the infection.</note>

If it were true bone, internal medicine could not dissolve it. But since it only *resembles* bone and is not true bone, why should it be difficult for internal treatment to dissolve it? 

Internally, one should use **Five Spirits Decoction** or the **Nine-Times Revolved Divine Pill**. <note>These are complex herbal formulas designed to "drain" the body of toxins. The "Five Spirits" often refers to a balance of five cooling and diuretic herbs.</note> These formulas drain the <term>Damp-Heat<gloss>the combination of inflammation and stagnant fluids that fuels the infection</gloss></term> without depleting the patient's <term>Qi and Blood<gloss>the fundamental energy and nutritive fluids required for the body to heal itself</gloss></term>. In this way, one does not even need to focus on "dissolving the bone"—the bone-like growths will dissolve on their own.

If you wish to achieve success even more rapidly, you may also use an external application. Take "flown" **Litharge** <note>original: *feiguo mituoseng*; Litharge is lead monoxide. "Flown" refers to the process of water-levigation, where the mineral is ground with water to produce an ultra-fine, purified powder.</note> and mix it with **Tung oil** to create a plaster. When spread upon the wound, this treatment and the internal medicine will complement each other, and the results will be swifter than ever.

<summary>This page describes the agonizing, chronic nature of bone abscesses where bone fragments continually surface, arguing that these are not "true bone" but products of infection that are best treated with internal herbal medicine rather than just physical extraction.</summary>
<keywords>osteomyelitis, sequestrum, internal medicine, Five Spirits Decoction, Litharge, Tung oil, Damp-Heat, Chen Shiduo</keywords>

# Lumbar Abscess
<note>original: *Yao Yong*. In traditional medicine, a "Yong" is a large, painful, deep-seated abscess or carbuncle involving localized swelling and tissue death.</note>

A lumbar abscess develops below the floating ribs near the **Belt Vessel** <term>Belt Vessel (*Dai Mai*): One of the 'Eight Extraordinary Vessels' in acupuncture that circles the waist like a belt, binding the other meridians together.</term>. It manifests specifically at the acupuncture points known as the **Pivot of Jade** <note>original: *Yushu* (GB27)</note> and **Maintaining the Path** <note>original: *Weidao* (GB28)</note>. This condition belongs to the **Gallbladder Meridian of Foot-Shaoyang** <term>Gallbladder Meridian: A pathway of vital energy (Qi) that runs along the side of the body; it is associated with the gallbladder's functions and the body's ability to resolve damp-heat.</term>.

When the abscess first appears, the pain is so excruciating that the patient cries out in agony, making it appear to be a **Yang syndrome** <note>In Chinese medicine, "Yang" syndromes are characterized by acute, hot, and "excess" conditions, such as high fever or intense inflammation.</note>. However, because the lower back and kidneys are the realm of **Ultimate Yin** <note>The kidneys are considered the deepest "Yin" organ, the root of the body's cooling and nourishing fluids.</note>, one must not treat this as a simple Yang condition.

This ailment is fundamentally born from the excessive suppression of **Essence** <term>Essence (*Jing*): The fundamental life force and reproductive energy stored in the kidneys.</term>—specifically, the desire to release essence (ejaculate) while holding back. This suppression causes the energy to stagnate and "brew" into **Fire-Toxin** <note>An internal heat pathogen that causes severe inflammation, pus formation, and tissue damage.</note>. In this state, it may appear to be a **pure Yin syndrome** <note>Yin syndromes are typically chronic, cold, or rooted in a deficiency of vitality.</note>. Yet, once the "fire" flares and the "toxin" is formed, there is Yang present within the Yin. Therefore, it cannot be treated using methods intended for pure Yin conditions.

It is best to treat both Yin and Yang simultaneously. If a physician does not supplement the Yin and only attempts to clear the Fire-Toxin, the **Kidney-Qi** <note>The functional energy of the kidneys.</note> will be further damaged, making the toxin even harder to resolve. Conversely, if one supplements the Yin but fails to supplement the Yang...

<summary>This page completes a recovery formula for bone abscesses and begins a discourse on lumbar abscesses, explaining that they are caused by suppressed sexual energy which creates a complex mix of Yin and Yang symptoms.</summary>
<keywords>Lumbar Abscess, Belt Vessel, Kidney-Qi, Fire-Toxin, Essence (Jing), Acupuncture points, Gallbladder Meridian, Chen Shiduo</keywords>

...[the root of the body's Yin fluids], then without Yang, life cannot be generated. Fire-toxin <term>Fire-toxin (*huodu*): A traditional concept describing intense pathogenic heat that causes inflammation, pus, and tissue destruction.</term> remains deeply hidden within the **Kidney Palace** <note>original: *Shengong*. A poetic and anatomical reference to the kidneys as the foundational seat of vitality.</note> and cannot be discharged externally. Only by combining treatment with the supplementation of Yin can one avoid the risk of a one-sided imbalance and achieve the wonderful effect of therapeutic connection.

# Arm Abscesses

When abscesses develop on both arms, one must carefully examine the **Naoshu** acupoint <note>Naoshu (SI10) is an acupuncture point located on the shoulder blade. Its name translates to "Upper Arm Transporter."</note>. This meridian belongs to the **Small Intestine channel of Great Yang** <note>original: *Taiyang Xiaochang*. In traditional Chinese medicine, this channel is associated with the exterior of the body and the processing of fluids.</note>. While this would seem to be a region that is not predominantly "Yin" in nature—and is generally considered less severe than abscesses on the back of the neck <note>original: *duikou*, a dangerous type of abscess located directly opposite the mouth on the nape of the neck</note> or the upper back—it can still be fatal if the treatment is not handled correctly. 

Therefore, it is essential to distinguish between their **Yang** and **Yin** qualities. Those that are painful and protrude high are Yang; those that are itchy and flat or sunken are Yin. For Yang syndromes, use the **Threefold Affirmation Decoction** <note>original: *Sanshi Tang*</note>; for Yin syndromes, use the **Abscess-Eliminating Yang-Returning Decoction** <note>original: *Xiaoyong Huanyang Tang*</note>. 

One cannot argue that because the hands and feet are not "core" organs <note>original: *fuxin*, literally "belly and heart," referring to the vital internal organs</note>, they only present Yang symptoms and never Yin ones. The limbs are governed by movement. If a **Yin Carbuncle** <term>Yin Carbuncle (*Yinju*): A deep-seated, cold-natured abscess that lacks redness and heat; these are often more difficult to treat because the body's vital energy is too weak to "push" the infection to the surface.</term> grows in a place of movement, then "movement" is transformed into "stillness." When movement turns to stillness, this signifies **Yang** gravitating toward **Yin**. Does this gravitation of Yang toward Yin not represent a transition from a state of life toward one of death?

Although the **Inner Canon** <note>original: *Neijing*, referring to the *Huangdi Neijing*, the foundational medical text of China.</note> states that "inducing perspiration will stop the sore," and it seems that arm abscesses could be treated by promoting sweat to allow the toxin to be expelled through the pores...

<summary>This page discusses arm abscesses, specifically noting their connection to the Small Intestine meridian and the vital importance of identifying whether the infection is Yang (active/protruding) or Yin (static/flat) to avoid fatality.</summary>
<keywords>arm abscess, Naoshu acupoint, Small Intestine meridian, Yang syndrome, Yin carbuncle, Huangdi Neijing, Threefold Affirmation Decoction</keywords>

...and dissipate them. However, **Yang abscesses** <term>Yang Yong: Acute, red, swollen, and painful sores that are generally easier to treat</term> can be dissipated through sweating, whereas **Yin abscesses** <term>Yin Yong: Deep-seated, cold, and slow-healing sores that lack redness and heat</term> must be dissipated through **tonification** <note>Tonification is the process of nourishing and strengthening the body's vital energy (Qi) and blood to help it expel the disease from within.</note>. Therefore, I have specifically brought this distinction to light.

### Knee Abscesses

An **abscess** <term>Yong: An acute, localized swelling with heat and redness</term> cannot actually form directly on top of the knee <note>The author refers specifically to the patella or kneecap, where the lack of thick muscle and flesh makes the formation of a traditional "fleshy" abscess impossible.</note>. What are called **Knee Abscesses** <note>original: *Xi Yong*</note> actually form on either the inner or outer sides of the joint.

The meridians and **acupuncture points** <term>Xue: Specific nodes along the body's energy pathways where Qi can be accessed</term> on the inner and outer sides of the knee are distinct from one another.

An abscess forming on the **outer side of the knee** involves the *Yangguan* <note>"Knee Yang Gate" (GB33)</note> and *Yanglingquan* <note>"Yang Mound Spring" (GB34)</note> points. This area belongs to the **Foot Shao-Yang Gallbladder Meridian**. An abscess in this location is specifically named a **Supporting Carbuncle** <note>original: *Tuo Ju*. These are often deep-seated and require the body's internal energy to "prop up" or push the toxins outward.</note>.

An abscess forming on the **inner side of the knee** involves the *Xuehai* <note>"Sea of Blood" (SP10)</note> and *Yinlingquan* <note>"Yin Mound Spring" (SP9)</note> points. This area belongs to the **Foot Tai-Yin Spleen Meridian**.

Although these two meridians are distinct, they share a common physiological trait: they are both "abundant in Qi but deficient in Blood" <note>In Traditional Chinese Medicine, different meridians have different ratios of vital energy (Qi) and physical nourishment (Blood). Because these meridians lack sufficient Blood to easily flush out toxins, infections here can be stubborn.</note>. Therefore, the treatment for both must prioritize **nourishing the Blood**, while using **Qi-tonification** as a secondary support to defeat the toxins. When the Blood is made to flourish, the toxins are easily expelled.

<summary>This page completes a formula for arm sores and introduces the diagnosis of knee abscesses, distinguishing between outer (Gallbladder meridian) and inner (Spleen meridian) infections.</summary>
<keywords>Knee Abscess, Supporting Carbuncle, Gallbladder Meridian, Spleen Meridian, White Atractylodes, Blood tonification, acupuncture points</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on Knee Abscesses from the previous page, specifically concluding the theoretical warning regarding the balance of "fire" and "vitality" before providing the primary medicinal formula, Full-Life Powder.</meta>

...is dispersed. If one focus solely on **draining fire** <note>This refers to using cooling herbs to clear inflammation and heat (fire).</note>, it will conversely damage the **Qi** <term>The body's vital life-force</term> and **Blood**. How then can the medicine achieve success and cause the wound to close? The knee is an area of extreme movement and serves as the **pivot** <note>original: *shuniu*; the essential turning point or axis</note> of the skeletal joints. Only when Qi and Blood are flourishing can one move. Can we afford to ignore the treatment of Qi and Blood?

I am therefore earnestly giving this warning. Generally, if there is swelling, redness, and pain, and after half a month there is pus that is yellow or white, the condition is treatable. If there is no pain or if the wound discharges fresh blood, it is a fatal sign. If the discharged pus is bluish-black, if multiple "heads" <note>Additional openings or drainage points on the skin.</note> gradually emerge, or if the abscess has no fixed location, it is incurable.

### Axillary Abscess <gloss>Including "Horse-Sword" scrofula and underarm lumps</gloss>

An axillary abscess <note>original: *yeyong*; a localized infection or carbuncle in the armpit.</note> originates at the **Tianchi** (Heavenly Pool) acupuncture point located beneath the arm. The *Tianchi* point belongs to the **Hand Jueyin Pericardium Meridian** <term>The energetic channel associated with the pericardium, the protective sac around the heart. In traditional theory, this channel regulates emotional stability and circulation.</term> This meridian is characterized by "abundant blood but sparse Qi." When a carbuncle or abscess develops here, it causes the patient to suffer from alternating chills and fever, intense pain, heat in the palms, and a sensation of tightness and redness in the arm.

Commonly, this is called a "clamped abscess" <note>original: *xieyong*</note> because the patient's arm appears to be "clamping" the toxic swelling against the side of the body. The *Lingshu* <note>The *Divine Pivot*, one of the most ancient and foundational texts of Chinese acupuncture and pathology.</note> states that if the swelling is hard and red, it is called a **Millet Abscess** <note>original: *miju*</note>. This may be treated with shallow lancing to allow the **fire-toxin** <note>Pathogenic heat and infection.</note> to escape the body. Because the essence of this fire-toxin is not deep, residing only between the layers of the skin and flesh, it can be cured through external piercing.

However, if one is negligent and allows the abscess to fester, its power will grow daily. There is a great danger that the fire-toxin will "enter the internal organs" <note>original: *ru zang*; a critical stage where a localized infection becomes systemic or affects vital organ functions.</note>, at which point the condition becomes difficult to treat. "Entering the organs" in this context refers to the toxin penetrating the two meridians of the **Liver and Spleen**. The Liver...

<summary>This page concludes the instructions for treating lower-extremity abscesses and introduces the "Axillary Abscess." It explains the anatomical and meridian-based origins of armpit swellings and warns of the dangers of internal organ involvement if left untreated.</summary>

<keywords>Axillary Abscess, Tianchi point, Pericardium Meridian, Fire-toxin, Lingshu (Divine Pivot), Millet Abscess, Yin Syndrome, Liver and Spleen meridians</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the pathology of axillary abscesses. While the previous page established the meridian involvement (the Pericardium channel), this text moves into the Spleen meridian's role and provides a specific medicinal formula for treatment.</meta>

When the blood in the meridians stagnates and the Qi of the Spleen meridian congeals, it is not easy to achieve success unless one supplements the Qi and blood while assisting with medicinal flavors that "internally disperse and externally draw out" <term>a therapeutic strategy (内疎外托, *neishu waituo*) that simultaneously clears internal toxins and encourages the discharge of pus or dissipation of swelling at the surface</term>. If the swelling is hard and does not rupture, it is classified as **"Horse-Sword" scrofula** <note>original: *madao*; long, hard, curved scrofulous lumps on the neck or armpit resembling the shape of a curved sword.</note> or **"Clamped Goiter"** <note>original: *xieying*; a lump or tumor-like swelling in the neck or axilla.</note>. These must also be treated urgently so that the toxicity can be dissolved. Otherwise, as months and years pass, once it fully erupts, it may become incurable <note>original: *chou*; to recover or heal from a chronic condition.</note>.

# Breast Abscess
<note>original: *Ruyong*</note>

When a breast swelling is at its largest, it is called **"Erupting Breast"** <term>original: *rufa*; an extensive, severe inflammation of the breast tissue resembling cellulitis</term>. When the swelling is somewhat smaller, it is called a **Breast Abscess** <term>original: *ruyong*; a localized collection of pus</term>. If, at the very beginning of the onset, a small sore-head appears, it is called a **Breast Scaly-Sore** <term>original: *rubi*; a superficial or early-stage lesion of the nipple or skin</term>. All three types of abscesses mentioned above cause a person to suffer from increased chills, high fever, and nausea.

<meta>This page continues the classification of breast-related pathologies begun on the previous page, detailing specific clinical presentations and distinguishing between types of inflammation based on patient history and physical signs.</meta>

# Classification of Breast Disorders

As for these conditions, if a woman who is pregnant but has not yet given birth experiences swelling and pain, it is called **Breast Blow** <note>original: *ru chui*; an acute inflammatory condition occurring during pregnancy</note>. If the swelling and pain occur after she has already delivered the child, it is called **Milk Blow** <note>original: *nai chui*; typically refers to acute mastitis during lactation</note>. Both of these conditions should be treated urgently to dissipate the inflammation; if treatment is delayed, it will inevitably lead to the formation of pus, which makes the condition much harder to cure.

In older women, when emotional stagnation and "pent-up heat" cause a nodule to form within the breast that does not dissipate and becomes painful during overcast or rainy weather, it is called a **Breast Nodule** <note>original: *ru he*</note>. If one follows the usual patterns of neglect and fails to treat it, the nodule will eventually break and ulcerate internally. If it then continuously drips pus and fluid and remains unhealed for a long duration, it is called a **Breast Fistula** <note>original: *ru lou*</note>.

When a childless woman out of maternal affection adopts an "adoptive child" <note>The original term *mingling* refers to the "caterpillar-hunting wasp," a classic literary metaphor for an adopted child</note> and forcefully allows the infant to suckle at her dry breasts, the prolonged irritation will cause the nipple to become sore and decay. If the nipple takes on an appearance resembling a lotus seed pod, it is called **Breast Canker** <note>original: *ru gan*</note>.

If, without any apparent cause, both breasts become as hard as stone and do not ulcerate even after several months, but cause frequent and persistent pain, it is called **Breast Rock** <term>original: *ru yan*; a historical term for breast cancer, so named because the tumors feel like hard stones</term>.

A red swelling on the breast that is round in shape but lacks a distinct "head" <note>A "head" refers to a visible point where an abscess is ready to rupture</note> is called a **Breast Abscess** <note>original: *ru yong*</note>.

The breast disorders mentioned above consist of approximately ten varieties. Generally, they are all categorized as **Yang Syndromes** <term>conditions characterized by heat, redness, and acute progression</term>. They are not like other types of abscesses that must be strictly categorized into "Yin" or "Yang" types to determine a treatment plan; rather, one must primarily distinguish between the stages of the disease to determine whether it is a state of **Vacuity or Repletion** <term>original: *xu shi*; "Vacuity" (Deficiency) refers to the weakness of the body's defenses, while "Repletion" (Excess) refers to the strength of the pathogenic infection</term>. 

Generally, when a breast abscess <note>original: *ru yong*</note> first emerges, it is mostly a state of **Pathogenic Repletion** <note>original: *xie shi*</note>. Once it has undergone a long period of ulceration...

<summary>This page classifies various breast ailments—including mastitis, nodules, and cancer—noting that most are "Yang" in nature and emphasizing the need to distinguish between early stages of infection and late-stage depletion.</summary>
<keywords>Breast Abscess (Ruyong), Breast Rock (Ruyan), Breast Nodule (Ruhe), Yang Syndromes, Vacuity and Repletion, clinical diagnosis, Chen Shiduo</keywords>

# Breast Abscess, Part Seven

Because the body’s upright resistance is deficient, the foolproof path to a total cure is to supplement the "center" <note>Referring to the Digestive Spleen and Stomach systems</note> while simultaneously dispersing the external pathogenic "evil." Regarding **Breast Abscesses** <term>ru yong: an acute inflammatory condition or abscess of the breast</term>, they are associated with the **Foot Yangming Stomach Meridian** <note>In traditional Chinese medicine, the breast tissue is governed by the Stomach meridian</note>, while the nipple itself is associated with the **Foot Jueyin Liver Meridian** <note>The Liver meridian is believed to pass through the nipples</note>. 

Furthermore, when a breast abscess develops, it is not necessarily the case that the Stomach meridian has an abundance of **Qi and Blood** <term>the vital energy and nourishing fluids of the body</term>, nor is it necessarily the case that the Liver meridian is not lacking in them. If one fails to supplement the **Qi and Blood** of these two meridians, the breast abscess will become stagnant and cannot be healed. One must not claim that this condition is purely **Yang** <note>characterized by heat and excess</note> and not **Yin** <note>characterized by cold and deficiency</note>, and focus solely on extinguishing "fire-toxins." To do so would result in the flesh being unable to regenerate and the sinews being unable to reconnect.

## *Jimen Yong*

### Collection of the Body: Volume Seven
->Seven<-

A **Jimen Abscess** <term>jimen yong</term> <note>Named after the Jimen acupuncture point, or "Sieve Gate"</note> develops on the inner thigh, specifically below the **Gate of Surging** <term>chongmen</term> point and above the **Sea of Blood** <term>xuehai</term> point. This area corresponds to the **Foot Taiyin Spleen Meridian**. This ailment is produced by the toxins of **Damp-Heat** <note>In traditional Chinese medicine, "Damp-Heat" refers to a pathological state characterized by inflammation, swelling, and fluid accumulation</note>. 

Because this meridian is characterized by an abundance of *Qi* <note>The body's vital energy or functional force</note> but a scarcity of Blood, the treatment should focus on "internal lifting" <note>A method of using tonifying herbs to push toxins outward from the deeper levels of the body</note>. It is appropriate to use a **Decoction of Astragalus and Bupleurum** <note>Huangqi Caihu Tang</note> supplemented with ingredients such as **Black Atractylodes** <term>cangzhu</term> and **Stephania Root** <term>fangji</term>. Externally, one should apply medicinal plasters; if treatment is administered according to the specific symptoms, success is not difficult to achieve. 

However, if one is not cautious regarding the illness and indulges in **bedroom labor** <note>A historical medical term for sexual intercourse, which was believed to deplete the "Kidney Essence" and weaken the body's ability to heal from sores</note>, the condition will transform into a **Yin Toxin**. In such cases, one must utilize **Warming Tonification** <note>A strategy using "warm" natured herbs to restore the body's fire and energy</note> to treat it.

If the swelling occurs above the *Jimen* point, specifically at the *Chongmen* point, it is called a **Brave Carbuncle** <term>yong ju</term>. This type is also swollen and hard. It typically ulcerates <note>Breaks open to discharge pus</note> within a month. If the resulting pus is yellowish-white, it is considered a **Yang Carbuncle** <note>Yang-type sores are characterized by redness, heat, and a vigorous immune response; they generally have a better prognosis than the cold, sunken "Yin" types</note> and can be treated. 

If the ulcerated hole is as large as a chicken egg, it is colloquially known as a **Fish-mouth** <term>yu kou</term> <note>Likely referring to an inguinal bubo or lymph node swelling in the groin, which often resembles the gasping mouth of a fish when it ulcerates</note>. These can appear as either single or double sores. If the wound fails to close after many years, it indicates that "Yang has transformed into Yin." In this state, nothing less than **Great Tonification** <note>The use of potent, high-dose restorative herbs like Ginseng and Astragalus to revive the body's depleted resources</note> will be sufficient to heal it.

This title usually refers to a Daoist deity or a legendary immortal physician, suggesting the recipe has a sacred or celestial origin.</note>.

This treatment is also miraculously effective.

### Eyebrow Abscess

An **Eyebrow Abscess** <term>meiju</term> develops between the eyebrows at the location of the **Yangbai** points <note>Acupuncture point GB14, "Yang White," located on the forehead above the midpoint of the eyebrow</note>. It causes red, burning, and elevated swelling extending from the eyebrow to the forehead.

The **Yangbai** point originally belongs to the **Gallbladder Meridian**, yet the Gallbladder and the **Liver** exist in an **Interior-Exterior relationship** <note>In Chinese medicine, these two organs are functionally paired; the Liver is the "yin" internal organ and the Gallbladder is its "yang" external partner</note>. When the Gallbladder is diseased, the Liver is also diseased. There has never been a Gallbladder abscess caused by **Fire-Toxin** <note>A term for virulent, heat-based inflammation</note> that did not also spread its harm to the Liver.

The Gallbladder Meridian is characterized by an abundance of *Qi* <note>The body's vital energy or functional force</note> but a scarcity of Blood, while the Liver Meridian has an abundance of Blood but a scarcity of *Qi*. When these two meridians are invaded by **Fire-Toxin**, it inevitably parches and dries up the *Qi* and Blood. Therefore, it is appropriate to treat both *Qi* and Blood simultaneously. For those cases where the swelling is as hard as stone...

## Wood-Borer Abscess

The **Wood-Borer abscess** <note>original: "蠹疽" (Dujue). The term "Dujue" refers to wood-boring insects or termites, suggesting a sore that hollows out or erodes the flesh from within.</note> is a sore that arises at the **Empty Basin** acupuncture point <note>original: "缺盆" (Quepen), also known as Stomach 12, located in the hollow above the collarbone.</note>. The Empty Basin point belongs to the **Foot-Yangming Stomach Meridian**. The Stomach is an "official mansion" (fu) characterized by an abundance of both **Blood and Qi**. Because of this, abscesses occurring at the Empty Basin are mostly **Yang syndromes** <note>Yang syndromes are typically characterized by heat, redness, swelling, and a rapid, "sturdy" progression.</note>. However, if a patient is not careful with the illness—failing to avoid anger and frustration or failing to cease **sexual overindulgence** <term>房勞 (fanglao): the depletion of "essence" and energy through excessive sexual activity, which weakens the body's ability to fight infection</term>—the condition will surely transform into a **Yin syndrome**. One must not mistakenly believe it is still a Yang syndrome and recklessly use fire-purging or toxin-dispelling medicines <note>Yin syndromes represent a state of depletion or "cold" infection; using harsh, "cold" cooling medicines on a Yin condition can further exhaust the patient's remaining vitality.</note>.

This is commonly known as the **Lifa Abscess**. It can be lanced on the tenth day; if lancing produces pus, it is a **Yang abscess**. If lancing produces no pus, it is a **Yin abscess**. It is popularly called the **Stone Furuncle** <note>original: "石疔" (shiding).</note>, a name that implies it is as hard as stone and does not respond to lancing. There is also a variation where, before the first "head" (the primary sore) has subsided, four or five more "heads" emerge, unequal in size like a **mother and her children**. This is also called **Scrofulous Sores** <note>original: "歷瘡" (lichuang).</note>. Although the outward momentum of these may seem mild, the underlying toxin is actually more severe. If the infection spreads to the heart, the patient will die. However, if there is white pus and the sore remains swollen without turning black or collapsing inward, there is still hope.

<summary>This page describes the Wood-Borer abscess (dujue) located at the Empty Basin (Quepen) point, emphasizing the danger of the condition shifting from a Yang to a Yin syndrome due to lifestyle factors. It provides diagnostic criteria for lancing and identifies the fatal "Stone Furuncle" and "Mother and Child" variations.</summary>
<keywords>Wood-Borer Abscess (Dujue), Empty Basin (Quepen), Stomach Meridian, Yang Syndrome, Yin Syndrome, Stone Furuncle (Shiding), Scrofulous Sores (Lichuang), Chen Shiduo</keywords>

->**Clumping Sores of the Fingers and Toes; Carbuncles of the Elbows and Wrists**<-

When "clumping sores" <term>疔 (ding): often translated as "felons" or "boils," these are small, hard, deep-rooted infections that are notoriously painful and can lead to sepsis.</term> occur on the fingers or toes, some may grow beside the fingertip; these are named **Snake Sores** <note>original: "蛇疔" (sheding). This likely refers to a digital felon or paronychia that wraps around the nail like a snake.</note>. Others grow atop the fingers or toes and are named according to the **Rat Condition**. <note>original: "鼠症" (shuzheng), possibly referring to the shape of the swelling or its resemblance to scrofulous nodes.</note> Generally, a sore that is highly swollen and painful is a **Yang Clumping Sore**, while one that is flat, swollen, and itchy [is a Yin Clumping Sore].
