# Seventy-Seven

## Volume 7: Sores of the Hands, Feet, and Fingers, with Gangrene Appended

This is a **Yin pattern** <note>original: "陰症" (*yinzheng*). In traditional Chinese medicine, Yin patterns are characterized by coldness, lack of pus, and a "sunken" nature, making them more dangerous and harder to treat than "Yang" (inflammatory) patterns.</note>. A **Yang pattern** must have pus, while a **Yin pattern** must not. Where there is pus, lancing the site will lead to recovery; where there is no pus, lancing it will only make the condition more severe. If there is no pus but the color is red, the patient will live. If there is no pus and the color is black, the patient will die—and this death is certain even if the blackness has not yet passed the joint.

There is a specific type where the blackness *does* pass the joint, occurring on the fingers or toes; this is called **Gangrene of the Extremities** <note>original: "脫疽" (*tuoju*), literally "the carbuncle where parts fall off." This matches the clinical description of gangrene or Buerger's disease.</note>, a name that implies the finger must be lost. 

This condition is most often contracted by those who indulge in **rich foods and fine grains** <note>original: "膏粱" (*gaoliang*), a term for a luxurious, sedentary lifestyle involving fatty meats and refined spirits that create "internal heat."</note> and who also employ **mineral elixirs and sexual arts** <term>alchemical drugs (often containing mercury or lead) and aphrodisiacs used to enhance libido or longevity</term>. Whether these medicines are held under the tongue, placed in the navel, applied to the vagina <gloss>the "yin gate"</gloss>, or rubbed on the penis <gloss>the "yang tool"</gloss>, the resulting **lewd fire** <note>pathological heat generated by overstimulation</note> runs wild, drying out the marrow within the bones. Accumulated over days and months, it eventually erupts as this carbuncle.

Now, the fingers and toes are the extremities of the four limbs; one would think toxins could not reach such distant points. Yet, who could have known that in the very places toxins are not supposed to reach, the toxic fluids fail to dissipate? Instead, they emerge from between the fingernails, where the poison is far more ferocious—ten times worse than in ordinary locations.

Is it then true that the best method of treatment must be to prioritize the surgical removal of the finger? This is not necessarily the case. The body’s **Vital Energy and Blood**... <margin>The text on the far left edge is from the book's fold and partially obscured.</margin>

<summary>Chen Shiduo explains the pathology of gangrene (Tuoju), attributing it to the exhaustion of bone marrow caused by a decadent lifestyle and the use of toxic alchemical aphrodisiacs. He distinguishes between treatable "red" Yin patterns and fatal "black" patterns of the disease.</summary>
<keywords>gangrene, Yin and Yang patterns, Tuoju, alchemical elixirs, sexual arts, bone marrow exhaustion, lewd fire, clinical prognosis</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on gangrene and sores of the limbs, transitioning from the systemic causes of these conditions to specific medicinal formulas for treatment.</meta>

If the circulation flows and reaches everywhere, then the toxic qi follows the flow and does not gather in one place. If the toxins must gather and turn red in a single spot, it is because they are taking advantage of a deficiency in **qi and blood** <term>the vital energy and nutritive fluid of the body, which must be in balance for health</term>. The emergence of widespread pox-like sores at the ends of the four limbs <note>original: "四條之末" (*si tiao zhi mo*), referring specifically to the hands and feet as the "ends" of the body's major pathways</note> occurs because the qi and blood cannot reach those extremities fully. What is this if not a state of **deficiency**? By greatly supplementing the qi and blood and adding ingredients that drain toxins, success is often achieved. How can one be saved without such precise and earnest treatment? Since these formulas cause no injury through pain and yet achieve the wonder of restoring life, I hope people will use them with faith.

# Sinew Carbuncles

<margin>
Carbuncles and gangrenous ulcers; Clove-like <unclear>toxin</unclear> <note>original: *Ding du*. This refers to "clove-sores" or boils that are small and hard like a nail, but highly toxic.</note>
</margin>

**Sinew Carbuncles** <term>original: *Jin ju*. A "Sinew" carbuncle refers to an infection affecting the tendons or ligaments of the foot</term> arise on the heels of both feet at the location of the **Kunlun** acupuncture point <note>Located in the depression between the external ankle bone and the Achilles tendon.</note>. **Fatigue Carbuncles** <note>original: *Lao ju*</note> arise behind the little toe at the location of the **Jinggu** and **Jinmen** acupuncture points <note>These points are located along the outer edge of the foot.</note>. If the sore arises behind the *Kunlun* point, it is also known as a **Foot Carbuncle**.

All of these conditions belong to the **Bladder Meridian of Foot Taiyang** <term>In Chinese medicine, "Meridians" are pathways through which vital energy (Qi) flows; the Bladder meridian runs down the back of the leg to the foot</term>. This meridian is characterized by having **abundant Blood but sparse Qi**. For a sinew carbuncle, if it ulcerates after five or six days and produces a yellow-white pus that is not too plentiful, the patient will be safe. However, if the color is black and the itching is intense, it is difficult to treat because the condition has transformed into a **Yin** state <note>A "Yin" state in this context suggests the body's vital energy is collapsing and the infection is turning "cold" and necrotic rather than "hot" and active.</note>. When a sinew carbuncle first begins...

<summary>This page provides the "Six Dings Decoction" for toe gangrene and begins a diagnostic guide for "Sinew Carbuncles" on the heel, emphasizing their relationship to the Bladder Meridian.</summary>

Within three to five days, the sensation is as if insects are gnawing at the flesh. If the condition persists for too long, maggots may even generate, and the wound may remain unhealed for years. This is known as a **"Curved Carbuncle"** <note>original: *Qu ju*. This likely refers to the way the infection causes the tendons to contract or "curve" the limb.</note> or a **"Cold Carbuncle"** <note>original: *Leng ju*. In traditional Chinese medicine, a "cold" sore is one that lacks redness and heat, signaling a chronic, stagnant, and difficult-to-treat "Yin" condition.</note>; these are both categorized as **"Yin sores."** One must use medicines that powerfully tonify the *Qi* and the Blood to benefit the patient, supplemented by ingredients that drain dampness and transform toxins. While some patients may survive, one cannot rely on these treatments to be effective if the condition has been allowed to linger for an extended period.

The **Foot Carbuncle** <term>original: *Zu ju*. A general term for severe infections or gangrene of the foot.</term> is also called the **"Beak Carbuncle."** If, at the onset, it is red and swollen with a distinct "head" that can be lanced, it is a **"Yang" condition** <note>original: *Yang zheng*. Unlike the "Cold/Yin" sores, "Yang" conditions are characterized by active inflammation, heat, and redness, which usually respond faster to treatment.</note>. If lancing it produces yellow or white pus, it will heal easily. However, if the sore breaks open immediately and appears black and necrotic, this is a most severe "Yin" condition. If left for too long, the foot may even fall off. It must be treated with great urgency; otherwise, the patient will not survive.

### Carbuncles of the Middle Courtyard and the Well

The **Middle Courtyard Carbuncle** <term>original: *Zhongting ju*. Named after the *Zhongting* (CV16) acupuncture point located on the sternum.</term> arises in the center of the chest between the breasts, located below the **Chest Center** point <note>original: *Shanzhong* (CV17). An acupuncture point considered the "mu-point" or gathering place of the body's Qi.</note>. The **Well Carbuncle** <term>original: *Jing ju*</term> arises at the **Turtledove Tail** point <note>original: *Jiuwei* (CV15). This point is located at the lower end of the sternum, near the xiphoid process; the name refers to the shape of the bone.</note>, which is situated further below the Middle Courtyard. Both of these acupuncture points belong to the **Conception Vessel** meridian <note>original: *Ren Mai*. This meridian runs up the anterior midline of the body and is fundamental to the "Yin" energy of the torso.</note>. The Conception Vessel is one of the Eight Extraordinary [Meridians]...

This is one variation of exhaustion. The **Conception Vessel** <term>original: *Renmai*. The primary "Yin" channel of the body, running up the midline of the torso.</term> originates at the **Meeting of Yin** <term>original: *Huiyin* (CV1), located at the perineum.</term>, and these two acupuncture points [the Middle Courtyard and the Well] are situated in close proximity to the Heart and the **Pericardium** <gloss>original: *Baoluo*</gloss>. This condition occurs when the Fire of the Heart and Pericardium blazes upward while the **Kidney Water** <note>In Chinese medicine, Kidney "Water" is the cooling, nourishing essence that balances the "Fire" of the Heart. Disease arises when this balance is lost.</note> is insufficient to provide relief. Therefore, over time, a carbuncle is born. 

Its shape resembles a large bean, and one must urgently use **internal supporting therapy** <note>original: *neituo*. A surgical principle in Chinese medicine using tonifying herbs to "push" a deep-seated infection toward the surface so it can drain.</note>. If not treated early within three or four days, death is certain by the tenth day. If the infection erupts outward, it is easily cured; if it erupts inward, it injures the internal membranes and leads to death.

# Volume 8: The Heyang Carbuncle

### The Heyang Carbuncle

The **Heyang Carbuncle** <term>original: *Heyang Ju*. *Heyang* (BL55) is an acupuncture point located on the back of the lower leg.</term> forms within the calf, below the **Middle of the Crease** <term>original: *Weizhong* (BL40), located at the center of the popliteal crease behind the knee.</term> and above the **Support the Sinews** <term>original: *Chengjin* (BL56)</term> point. This is the precise location of the **Heyang** <note>literally "Confluence of Yang"</note> acupuncture point.

The *Heyang* point belongs to the **Foot Greater Yang Bladder Meridian** <note>The longest meridian in the body, running from the eyes, over the head, down the back, and ending at the little toe; it is traditionally associated with the body's exterior defenses.</note>. This condition is caused by the influence of **Damp-Heat** <note>A diagnostic pattern in Chinese medicine where internal moisture and inflammatory "heat" combine, leading to swelling, redness, and the formation of toxins.</note> which steams and congeals into a toxin; over time, this results in the birth of a carbuncle.

In the initial stages, one should use **internal supporting therapy** <note>original: *neituo*. A surgical principle in Chinese medicine where tonifying herbs are used to strengthen the patient's energy to "push" a deep-seated infection toward the surface.</note> to eliminate dampness and clear heat, thereby inducing sweating so that the toxin may be expelled through the perspiration. However, if the carbuncle's shape has already fully formed, inducing sweat is no longer appropriate. At that stage, one must discharge the pus and stop the pain to encourage the growth of new flesh.

### Two Golds Heat-Draining Decoction
->Transmitted by the Perfected Lord Wu Peng for treating carbuncles on the calf<-

### Scabies

Half of all occurrences of **scabies** <term>original: *Jiechuang*. While modern medicine defines scabies as a specific parasitic infestation, in this historical context, it refers to a broad category of itchy, eruptive skin sores and contagious rashes.</term> occur in people of wealth <note>original: *gaoliang ren*, literally "people of grease and grain," referring to those who consume a rich, fatty, and high-status diet.</note>. This is entirely due to the stagnation of the **Nutritive and Defensive systems** <term>original: *Ying Wei*. In Chinese medicine, *Ying* (Nutritive Qi) flows within the vessels to nourish the body, while *Wei* (Defensive Qi) flows outside the vessels to protect the surface. When these "circulate and halt" (stagnate), health breaks down.</term> and the outward eruption of **fire-toxin** <note>A term for intense inflammatory heat that manifests as redness, swelling, and pain on the skin.</note>. 

It is not only caused by failing to follow the seasonal changes of cold and warmth, or by the sudden disharmony of the **killing air** <note>original: *xiaosha*. This refers to the harsh, astringent energy associated with autumn which, if it strikes a body out of balance, can cause sudden illness.</note> of the seasons. Consequently, the locations where these sores appear do not follow fixed positions on the body. 

The symptoms of this condition are quite numerous, and their names have differed between ancient and modern times. **The Perfected Man Sun** <note>Sun Simiao (581–682 CE), the "King of Medicine," a legendary physician of the Tang Dynasty.</note> classified fifteen types; **Li Dongyuan** <note>Li Gao (1180–1251 CE), one of the four great masters of the Jin-Yuan dynasties, famous for his work on the Spleen and Stomach.</note> classified twenty-three types; and **Shen Douyuan** <note>A famous Ming dynasty surgeon known for his comprehensive work on external diseases, the *Qiyuanwaike*.</note> classified thirty-four types. In truth, Hua Yuan... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence, likely referring to the legendary surgeon Hua Tuo.</note>

### Furuncles

These manifestations are divided into five types, which encompasses them all. These five types are categorized according to the **Five Viscera** <term>original: *Wuzang*. The five primary yin organs: Heart, Liver, Spleen, Lung, and Kidney.</term>. The reason there are so many different names for these conditions is that they were named based on their physical appearance <note>Literally "named by following the image," a practice where a boil might be named after its resemblance to a fruit, animal, or object.</note>. Having an excess of names actually leads to a lack of fixed treatment methods. It is better to follow the "Five Furuncles of **Yuanhua**" <note>Yuanhua is the courtesy name of Hua Tuo, a legendary physician of the Han Dynasty known for his expertise in surgery and external medicine.</note> as the essential guide.

Generally, if the shape and color of a **furuncle** <term>original: *Ding*. A "nail-like" boil characterized by its deep, hard root and potential for rapid, dangerous progression.</term> are red, it is a **Heart Furuncle**; if the color is white, it is a **Lung Furuncle**; if the color is blue-purple, it is a **Liver Furuncle**; if the color is yellow, it is a **Spleen Furuncle**; and if the color is black, it is a **Kidney Furuncle**. By using the five colors to identify the Five Viscera, and using the Five Viscera to distinguish the five types of furuncles, and then treating them according to these specific categories to achieve recovery, how could there be any error?

Nevertheless, the shape, color, and symptoms of each furuncle must be clearly detailed. 

For instance, the **Heart Furuncle** is commonly known as the "Flame Furuncle" <note>original: *Huoyan Ding*. Named for its rapid onset and fiery red appearance.</note>. It arises at the ends of the Heart channel's **Shu, Mu, or Jing-well points** <term>Specific categories of acupuncture points where the energy of the Heart organ is said to gather, alarm, or emerge at the extremities.</term>, or it may appear on the lips, the mouth, or the palm and little finger of the hand. 

It begins as a single small red or yellow blister that throbs and is exceptionally itchy and painful. There is numbness in the limbs on both sides; in severe cases, the patient experiences chills and fever, irritability, mental confusion, dizziness, blurred vision, and restless sleep. Their speech becomes incoherent, urine is scanty, the face is red, and they suffer from thirst. The tongue... <note>The text ends mid-sentence describing the appearance of the tongue.</note>

As for the **Liver Furuncle** <term>original: *Ganding*. A deep-rooted boil associated with the liver system.</term>, its colloquial name is the **Purple Swallow Furuncle** <note>original: *Ziyanding*. Likely named for its dark, purplish color resembling the feathers of a swallow.</note>. It manifests in areas corresponding to the liver organ, such as the tip of the big toe, the region of the ribs and flanks, or within the tendons and bones. When it first emerges, it forms a blue-purple blister. On the second day, it breaks, discharging bloody fluid. After three days, the infection spreads through the tendons and causes the bone to rot, resulting in agonizing pain and suffering.

In severe cases, the eyes become red and vision grows dim, the fingernails turn a pure blue-green, and the patient suffers from alternating fever and chills. The head and neck are entirely painful, the mouth tastes bitter, and there is pain in the flanks. Urination becomes difficult and painful, the tongue feels stiff, the spirit is clouded, and the patient may experience **delirious speech** <term>original: *zhanyu*. Incoherent rambling often caused by high heat or severe internal imbalance.</term> and be easily startled. This is a disease originating in the **Liver Meridian**.

As for the **Spleen Furuncle** <term>original: *Piding*.</term>, its colloquial name is the **Yellow Drum Furuncle** <note>original: *Huangguding*. Named for its distinct yellow color and the tight, drum-like swelling of the skin.</note>. It manifests in areas corresponding to the spleen organ, or occurs at the corners of the mouth, the cheeks, the cheekbones, the upper and lower eyelids, and the **temporal region** <term>original: *Taiyang*. The area of the temples and the forehead.</term> or the front of the face. When it first emerges, it appears as a yellow blister that is bright, clear, and moist. The four sides are surrounded by a persistent red inflammation that does not dissipate. It may feel numb or itchy, and the skin feels tight, swollen, and hard.

The symptoms include an inability to eat and alternating fever and chills. In severe cases, there is nausea, vomiting, numbness and pain in the limbs, a feeling of irritability and oppression in the chest, and **dry heaving** <term>original: *ganhuo*. Retching or the urge to vomit without producing any substance.</term>. This is a disease originating in the **Spleen Meridian**.

### Volume 8

The **Lung Furuncle** <term>original: *Feiding*. A deep-rooted, "nail-like" boil associated with the Lung system.</term>, colloquially known as the **Bletilla Furuncle** <note>original: *Baijiding*. Likely named after the white Bletilla orchid root, reflecting the white color of the blister.</note>, grows along the areas and meridians of the Lung. It usually begins on the thumb. At its first appearance, it is a white blister with a hard top and a deep-set root. When it breaks, it discharges a fatty fluid. The itching and pain are unbearable. It tends to rot easily and sink inward <note>The "sinking" of a boil was considered a dangerous sign in traditional surgery, indicating the toxin was moving from the skin to the internal organs.</note>. This furuncle causes fever and coughing. In severe cases, the cheeks are damaged and the throat feels scorched; the hair stands on end and the skin is feverish. The patient spits up thick phlegm, the nose appears pinched, and breathing is rapid. This is a disease resulting from changes within the **Lung Meridian** <term>original: *Feijing*. The energetic pathway starting from the chest and ending at the thumb.</term>.

As for the **Kidney Furuncle** <term>original: *Shending*.</term>, colloquially known as the **Black Mole Furuncle** <note>original: *Heiyeding*. *Heiye* refers to a dark spot or mole; here it describes the blackened, necrotic appearance of the infection.</note>, it mostly grows along the pathways of the Kidney Meridian. It may appear near the ear opening, the chest, abdomen, waist, or the kidney region, or in more remote areas. It may also grow on the small toe or the **Gushing Spring point** <note>original: *Yongquan*. The first point of the Kidney Meridian (KI1), located on the sole of the foot.</note>. 

At its first appearance, there are black spots or purple blisters, and the toxin threads through the skin, gradually attacking the muscles and flesh. It becomes as hard as stone, and the pain penetrates into the bone marrow. Its fever and chills are irregular; the condition is light during the day but heavy at night. The complexion is a dim, sooty black. In severe cases, the hands and feet turn blue-purple, and the patient suffers from palpitations and heavy lethargy. The boil becomes soft and sinks, forming a deep hole, and secretions leak from the eyes. This is a disease that arises from the **Kidney Meridian** <term>original: *Shenjing*. The energetic pathway running from the sole of the foot up the inner leg to the torso.</term>. Therefore, when one sees a black color, they know it is a Kidney Furuncle. To treat it, one must focus on the Kidneys and add ingredients that detoxify and "remove fire" <note>In this context, "fire" refers to pathological heat or inflammation that consumes the body's fluids.</note>. When one sees the color...

<meta>This page continues the diagnostic classification of the "Five Furuncles" (*Wuding*), which are deep-seated, "nail-like" boils. It explains how to align the color of the sore with the corresponding internal organ (Five Viscera) to determine the treatment, and begins a more detailed list of specific furuncle variations, starting with the Sesame Seed Furuncle.</meta>

If the color is **yellow**, one knows it is a **Spleen Furuncle** <term>original: *Piding*. In the Five Elements system, yellow corresponds to the Spleen and the Earth element.</term>. Treat the Spleen and add medicinal ingredients that detoxify and clear fire <note> "Clearing fire" (*quhuo*) refers to using "cold" or "cool" herbs to reduce the inflammation and heat associated with the infection.</note>. If the color seen is **white**, one knows it is a **Lung Furuncle** <term>original: *Feiding*. White corresponds to the Lung and the Metal element.</term>. Treat the Lung and add medicinal ingredients that detoxify and clear fire. If the color seen is **bluish-purple**, one knows it is a **Liver Furuncle** <term>original: *Ganding*. Green/blue corresponds to the Liver and the Wood element.</term>. Treat the Liver and add medicinal ingredients that detoxify and clear fire. If the color seen is **bright red**, one knows it is a **Heart Furuncle** <term>original: *Xinding*. Red corresponds to the Heart and the Fire element.</term>. Treat the Heart and add medicinal ingredients that detoxify and clear fire. Following this logic, how could any furuncle fail to be dissipated? 

Yet, fearing that the colors of the Five Furuncles may not be entirely reliable on their own, I shall further list the names of various specific furuncles below, to facilitate the differentiation of symptoms for the people of this world.

The **Sesame Seed Furuncle** <term>original: *Maziding*. Named for the small, seed-like appearance of the pustules.</term>: Its shape consists of protruding flesh with "heads" as numerous as grains of broomcorn millet or wheat. The color is slightly black, its four edges are faintly red, and it is extremely itchy. This is also a variety of **Kidney Furuncle** <term>original: *Shending*. Black corresponds to the Kidney and the Water element, explaining the dark color of this specific sore.</term>.

The **Male Furuncle** <term>original: *Xiongding*</term>: Its appearance is a pure, deep black. On its four sides, blisters rise up, filled with a yellow-colored fluid. It is roughly the size of the hole in a copper coin <note>Traditional Chinese coins had a square hole in the center; this is a common historical reference for size and shape.</note>, and the "neck" or base of the sore is high and protruding. This is also a **Kidney Furuncle**.
<column-break/>
The **Female Furuncle** <term>original: *Ciding*</term>: Its appearance is slightly yellow and it feels inflamed and burning deep within. It resembles the sore left by a moxibustion treatment <note>Moxibustion involves burning mugwort on the skin, often leaving a small, circular, cauterized wound.</note>. While blisters rise on all four sides, the center is concave and red, shaped like the hole of a coin. This is a **Spleen Furuncle** <term>original: *Piding*. The Spleen corresponds to the Earth element and the color yellow.</term>.
<column-break/>
The **Fire Furuncle** <term>original: *Huoding*</term>: Its shape looks as if it were scalded by boiling water or scorched by fire. The head of the sore is black and inflamed, with a smoky, cloudy fluid <note>original: *yanjiang*. This likely refers to a dark or turbid discharge from the blisters.</note> at its four edges. It also looks like grains of red millet. One must strictly **avoid moxibustion or cauterization** for this type. This is a **Heart Furuncle** <term>original: *Xinding*. The Heart corresponds to the Fire element and the color red.</term>.
<column-break/>
The **Rotten Furuncle** <term>original: *Landing*</term>: Its shape and color are somewhat black with white spots. The sore eventually ulcerates and flows with pus. It varies in size, sometimes appearing as large as the surface of a spoon. This is also a **Kidney Furuncle**.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of specific furuncle types (ding), describing their unique physical characteristics and their corresponding internal organs within the Five Viscera system. The **Snake-Head Furuncle** <term>original: *Shetouding*. Also known as the **Snake-Eye Furuncle** (*Sheyanding*).</term>: Its shape is like that of a snake's head, featuring two "eyes" that resemble a serpent’s gaze. It causes immense and frequent pain. These typically grow on the tips of the fingers or toes. In treatment, it is appropriate to first remove the "eye-filaments" <note>The "eye-filaments" (*yanxi*) refer to the fibrous roots or the core of the infection that were thought to anchor the sore.</note> and only then apply the medicinal ointment. This is also a **Kidney Furuncle** <term>original: *Shending*. The Kidney is associated with the Water element and deep-seated, painful conditions.</term>.

<column-break/>

The **Salt-Skin Furuncle** <term>original: *Yanfuding*</term>: Its appearance resembles the surface of a spoon. The four edges are entirely red, and it is dotted with black, millet-like grains. One must strictly avoid eating salt while afflicted. This is a **Heart Furuncle** <term>original: *Xinding*. In traditional Chinese medicine, the Heart corresponds to the Fire element and the color red; salt, belonging to the Water element, is believed to suppress or "check" the Heart's energy.</term>.

<column-break/>

The **Water-Wash Furuncle** <term>original: *Shuixiding*</term>: Its shape is similar to a coin and it features a central hole. The head appears white while the interior is black; pus discharges from the center, which remains hard to the touch. One must avoid drinking water or washing the area with water. This is a **Lung Furuncle** <term>original: *Feiding*. The Lung corresponds to the Metal element and the color white, and is responsible for the body's "water passages."</term>.

<column-break/>

The **Blade-Buckwheat Furuncle** <term>original: *Daoqiaoding*</term>: Its width and length resemble a buckwheat leaf, measuring about one inch <note>A traditional Chinese *cun*, often translated as an inch, is approximately 3.3 centimeters or 1.3 inches.</note> in size. It is black in color, appearing as if the skin has been cauterized or scorched by fire. One must strictly avoid the use of needles or knives to pierce or cut it; it should be treated solely with herbal medicine. This is also a **Kidney Furuncle**.

<summary>This page describes four specific types of furuncles (Snake-Head, Salt-Skin, Water-Wash, and Blade-Buckwheat), detailing their visual symptoms, organ correspondences, and the specific dietary or procedural taboos required for their cure.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the diagnostic catalog of furuncles (ding), further categorizing them by their resemblance to animals or objects. It links these external symptoms to the Five Viscera (the Spleen, Liver, Heart, and Lungs) and specifies dietary restrictions (avoiding the meat of the animal the sore resembles) which was a common principle in Ming dynasty pathology.</meta>

The **Floating Bubble Furuncle** <term>original: *Fu’ouding*</term>: Its shape is curved and nearly round, with a small gap where the ends do not quite meet. It is long and narrow, roughly the size of a shallot leaf <note>original: *xieye*. The leaf of the Chinese shallot (*Allium chinense*) is narrow, long, and hollow; this comparison describes a slender, slightly raised lesion.</note>. The interior is black while the exterior is yellow. If one pricks the black area, it is painless; however, the yellow area is painful. This is also classified as a **Wild Furuncle** <term>original: *Yeding*. A general term for virulent sores that appear on the extremities or are not yet rooted in a specific internal organ system.</term>.

<column-break/>

The **Ox-Constraint Furuncle** <term>original: *Niujuding*</term>: Its appearance is the color of flesh, rising up like a blister. It will not break even if pinched. There are no dietary prohibitions associated with it. Even if it is not treated, it will not kill the patient. This is a **Spleen Furuncle** <term>original: *Piding*. In traditional medicine, the Spleen governs the muscles and flesh; because this sore is "flesh-colored" and mild, it is linked to Spleen energy.</term>. It is the mildest of all furuncle types.

<column-break/>

The **Pig Furuncle** <term>original: *Zhuding*</term>: Its shape is round and small, featuring a weeping opening <note>original: *shenkou*. A "seeping" or "weeping" pore from which fluid or pus may drain.</note>. Inside the opening, there is a red filament <note>original: *hong*. Literally "swan," but here likely a shorthand for a red, thread-like core or "root" of the infection.</note>. One must avoid eating pork. This is a **Liver Furuncle** <term>original: *Ganding*</term>.

<column-break/>

The **Ox Furuncle** <term>original: *Niuding*</term>: Its shape is round with a weeping opening. The interior is marked by "Fire" <note>original: *bing*. The third Heavenly Stem, associated with Yang Fire, suggesting an intense, burning heat within the lesion.</note> and lacks any oily pus. It rises like a blister and will not break when pinched. It causes the patient to suffer from chills and fever. One must avoid eating beef. This is a **Lung Furuncle** <term>original: *Feiding*</term>.

<column-break/>

The **Dog Furuncle** <term>original: *Gouding*</term>: Its shape is elongated and pointed, and its color is red. It causes chills and fever. One must avoid eating dog meat. This is a **Heart Furuncle** <term>original: *Xinding*. The Heart corresponds to the Fire element and the color red.</term>.

<column-break/>

The **Sheep Furuncle** <term>original: *Yangding*</term>: Its shape is elongated and its color is white. It is accompanied by chills and fever. One must avoid eating mutton. This is a **Lung Furuncle** <term>original: *Feiding*. In the Five Elements system, the Lungs correspond to the Metal element and the color white.</term>.

The **Donkey-Horse Furuncle** <term>original: *Lvmading*</term>: Its shape resembles three moons <note>original: *sanyue*. This likely refers to a cluster of three crescent-shaped lesions or a single lesion with three distinct curved points.</note>. On the top of the sore, there are black dots, while the root or base is red in color. The top is protruding, and the condition is accompanied by alternating chills and fever. The patient must avoid eating donkey or horse meat. This is also classified as a **Kidney Furuncle** <term>original: *Shending*. In the system of the Five Viscera, the Kidneys are associated with the color black; the black dots on this sore signal Kidney involvement.</term>.

The **Melon-Vine Furuncle** <term>original: *Guatengding*</term>: These appear in countless numbers. Their shape is long and round, resembling a melon. They are caused by the "poison" of melons and are triggered by eating them. The patient must avoid eating melons. This is also a **Kidney Furuncle**.

The **Tofu Furuncle** <term>original: *Doufuding*</term>: Its appearance is that of a white blister. Within three days, the center of the top will sink inward. It is caused by eating tofu that has been contaminated by human sweat during its preparation <note>A common folk belief in Ming-era medicine was that the salty "essence" of human sweat reacted poorly with the cooling nature of tofu, creating a specific toxicity.</note>. Wheat gluten <term>original: *mianjun*</term> can cause the same reaction. This is a **Lung Furuncle** <term>original: *Feiding*. The Lungs are associated with the color white and the skin/pores; hence, a white blister that "sinks" (collapses like breath) is linked to Lung energy.</term>.

The **Qi Furuncle** <term>original: *Qiding*</term>: Its shape varies between large and small. The blister is white, as if filled with air or vapor. It is born from experiencing the internal "Qi" <note>Energy or emotional state.</note> of anger or grief. One must avoid anger. This is also a **Lung Furuncle**, as the poison within the Lungs acts to restrict the **Liver-Wood** <term>original: *Ganmu*. In the Five Elements cycle, Metal (Lungs) normally controls Wood (Liver). Here, the "poison" in the Lungs over-suppresses the Liver, which governs the flow of emotions like anger.</term>.

The **Ghost Furuncle** <term>original: *Guiding*</term>: Its shape also varies in size, and its color is a bluish-green. It is born from being struck by "evil poisonous Qi" <note>original: *xie du zhi qi*. This refers to external environmental pathogens or sudden, unexplained illnesses that were historically attributed to supernatural or "spectral" influences.</note>. It differs from the other... <note>The text terminates here due to the cropping of the page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the diagnostic catalog of acute sores, moving from the Lung-related "Tofu Furuncles" on the previous page to those affecting the Liver, Heart, and deeper internal systems. The text maintains its focus on visual morphology and the theory of the Five Viscera.</meta>

### Furuncles
<note>original: *Dīng* (疔). These are acute, deep-rooted, and potentially life-threatening inflammatory sores, often compared to the head of a nail in Chinese medical literature.</note>

The **Qi Furuncle** <term>original: *Qiding*</term>: This sore becomes significantly worse at night. It causes the patient to speak incoherently, as if they are seeing ghosts or apparitions. This is a **Liver Furuncle** <term>original: *Ganding*. In traditional theory, the Liver is associated with the "ethereal soul" and vision; when toxic heat disturbs the Liver, it manifests as hallucinations or nocturnal delirium.</term>.

The **Red Thread Furuncle** <term>original: *Hongsiding*</term>: Its shape consists of thin, trailing strands that resemble silk threads. These lines wrap around and travel across the body. If they appear on the hands or feet and extend inward toward the chest, the patient will die immediately as the toxin "enters the Heart" <note>This accurately describes lymphangitis—red streaks indicating a spreading infection. Without modern antibiotics, such systemic infections reaching the heart/bloodstream were frequently fatal.</note>. It is appropriate to use a pine needle <note>A traditional tool for bloodletting, chosen for its perceived "cleansing" or "cooling" nature compared to metal.</note> to prick the sore and draw out the blood. The patient must strictly avoid eating "hot" or spicy foods. This is a **Heart Furuncle** <term>original: *Xinding*</term>.

**Internal Furuncles** <term>original: *Neiding*</term>: These refer to sores that grow in the armpits, on the ribs, on the inner side of the shins, or inside the throat and mouth. These are fundamentally different from external sores and are especially dangerous and harmful. These are known as **Furuncles of the Five Viscera** <term>original: *Wuzang zhi ding*; signifying that the "poison" has originated deep within the primary organs rather than on the surface.</term>.

The **Grape Furuncle** <term>original: *Putaoding*</term>: Its shape is black with a purple tint, appearing bright and translucent like a crystal; hence its name. The black blood and toxic fluid inside the blister must be removed. This is also classified as a **Kidney Furuncle** <term>original: *Shending*. The Kidney is associated with the color black and the element of water; the dark, fluid-filled nature of this sore indicates Kidney involvement.</term>.

The **Bayberry Furuncle** <term>original: *Yangmeiding*</term>: Its shape is dark purple, resembling a smoked plum. If a patient has "plum sores" <term>original: *meichuang*, a common Ming-dynasty term for syphilis</term> over their entire body, but these one or two specific furuncles are present, the rest of the plum sores will not erupt or resolve. One must use a needle to prick the poisonous furuncle to allow the toxin to leak out; only then will the plum sores begin [to resolve]...

The **Fish Navel Furuncle** <term>original: *Yuqiding*</term>: Its shape resembles the navel of a fish. These most often grow on the fleshy "belly" of the forearms or the calves.

**Pox Furuncles** <term>original: *Douding*</term> come in different sizes. If these small furuncles suddenly appear during an outbreak of smallpox <note>original: *Dou* (痘). In this context, it refers to the pustules of smallpox or similar eruptive viral diseases.</note>, the pox sores over the entire body will fail to erupt or ripen properly. The **Centipede Furuncle** <term>original: *Wugongding*</term>: Its shape is long like a centipede, appearing to have a head and legs. It causes the patient to suffer from chills and fever. Although it is said to be contracted by eating food that a centipede has crawled over, it is fundamentally caused by **Fire Toxin** <term>original: *Huodu*; intense pathogenic heat that causes inflammation and tissue destruction</term> residing in the Lungs. Treatment should focus on the Lungs, adding medicinal flavors that detoxify and clear Fire. Externally, apply **Realgar** <term>original: *Xionghuang*; an arsenic sulfide mineral used in ancient medicine to "draw out" toxins</term> pellets or mashed **slugs** <term>original: *Yanyou*</term>, and the condition will resolve naturally.

The **Starry Sky Furuncle** <term>original: *Mantianxingding*</term>: Its shape is black and raised, resembling a black bean. It is surrounded on all four sides by a red hue. Today, one lesion appears...

If on the next day two more lesions appear, and they continue to increase daily until they reach the number of thirty-six, and then no more appear, this is also a **Kidney Furuncle** <term>original: *Shending*</term>. Its toxin is the most violent and the furuncle itself is the most ferocious. It must be treated early; if the count reaches thirty-six, even if one possessed a **Divine Elixir** <note>Reference to legendary alchemical medicines capable of curing any ailment or granting immortality.</note>, there would be no way to save the patient.

For all the various furuncles described above, one must strictly avoid **sexual intercourse** <note>In traditional Chinese medicine, sexual activity was believed to deplete "Essence" (Jing), which is stored in the Kidneys and is vital for fighting off "toxic heat" and inflammation.</note>. If this rule is violated even once, a mild case will become severe, and a severe case will result in death.

# Immortal Chrysanthemum Drink
<term>original: *Xianju Yin*</term>

Transmitted by **True Lord Wu Peng** <note>A mythical physician-shaman of high antiquity, often cited in Daoist medical texts as a source of divine healing methods.</note> for the treatment of furuncles involving extreme pain; it is effective for all varieties of furuncles.

<column-break/>

Every case has proven successful.

<column-break/>

**Chrysanthemum** (roots and leaves used together) <gloss>two taels</gloss>, **Raw Licorice Root** <gloss>three mace, ground into powder</gloss>. Pound the chrysanthemum roots and leaves.

Take the resulting juice and mix in the raw licorice powder. Drink it; recovery begins the moment it passes the lips.

# Powder for Restoration
<term>original: *Bahui San*; literally "Powder to Pull Back [the Soul]"</term>
Specifically for treating furuncle poison <note>A category of deep, hard, and highly dangerous boils often accompanied by systemic symptoms like fever or chills.</note>; it brings the dying back to life.

**Frankincense** <note>The resin of *Boswellia* trees, used to invigorate blood and ease pain.</note> <gloss>one mace, ground raw</gloss>, **Blue Vitriol** <note>Also known as Chalcanthite; a mineral used topically in small amounts to "eat" away infected or necrotic flesh.</note> <gloss>one mace, ground raw</gloss>, **Catechu** <gloss>one mace</gloss>, **Borneol** <note>A pungent, cooling substance derived from the resin of the *Dryobalanops aromatica* tree, used to reduce swelling.</note> <gloss>one mace</gloss>, **Musk** <note>A highly aromatic substance used to "open" the body's channels and allow medicine to penetrate the deep-seated root of a boil.</note> <gloss>one mace</gloss>, **Dragon Bone** <note>Fossilized bones of prehistoric mammals, often used in ancient medicine to stabilize the body and promote the closing of wounds.</note> <gloss>one mace</gloss>. 

Grind all together into a fine powder and store in a porcelain vessel. When a furuncle first appears, prick the "head" of the sore open and place this powder into the opening; the poison will immediately dissipate.

# Powder for Guarding Against Furuncles
<term>original: *Fangding San*</term>
Treats furuncles when the condition is not yet spreading widely; it will cause them to vanish immediately.

**Siler Root** <note>Botanic: *Saposhnikovia divaricata*; used to "dispel wind" and toxins from the surface of the body.</note> <gloss>one mace</gloss>, **Raw Licorice** <gloss>eight fen (approx. 3g)</gloss>, **Honeysuckle** <gloss>one mace</gloss>, **Forsythia** <gloss>one mace</gloss>, **Violet** <note>Botanic: *Viola philippica*; historically considered the most specific herb for treating the "root" of furuncles.</note> <gloss>one mace five fen</gloss>, **Trichosanthes Root** <note>Known in the text as *Tianhua Fen* (Heavenly Flower Powder); it clears heat and generates fluids to counteract the dryness caused by infection.</note> <gloss>one mace</gloss>, **Raw Rehmannia Root** <gloss>two mace</gloss>, **Figwort Root** <gloss>one mace</gloss>, **Red Peony Root** <gloss>five fen</gloss>.

Add two bowls of water and boil the mixture until only eight-tenths of a bowl remains. Take while warm.

## Puncturevine Powder

## Bone-Envy Sores

"Bone-Envy Sores" <note>original: *Guxian Chuang*; a descriptive name for a chronic, pruritic (itchy) skin condition where the patient scratches so deeply they seem to "envy" the bone.</note> manifest at the two *Shentang* [Spirit Hall] acupuncture points, or at the *Geguan* [Diaphragm Pass] or *Yixi* points <note>These are all acupuncture points located on the upper back, situated along the meridian of the bladder.</note>. Although these points belong to the Greater Yang Bladder Meridian <term>original: *Taiyang Pangguang Jing*; the longest meridian in the body, primarily associated with the body's exterior defenses</term>, which would suggest this is a disease of the "Yang" (exterior or active) channels, this particular ailment behaves differently. 

If this sore does not erupt, it causes no trouble; however, once it manifests, there is never a case where it is not intensely itchy. According to medical principles, the pain of a sore indicates that the toxin is erupting from the Yang; conversely, the itch of a sore indicates that the toxin is erupting from the Yin <note>In traditional diagnosis, pain is often seen as an "excess" or "heat" (Yang) symptom, while chronic, deep itching is associated with "deficiency" or "blood dryness" (Yin).</note>. Extreme itchiness represents the absolute peak of Yin. The "Bone-Envy Sore" is defined precisely by this extremity of itching. 

The itch is so unbearable that the sufferer cannot help but scratch to find a moment of relief. However, there is no remedy in this: the more one scratches, the more it itches, and the more it itches, the more one scratches. If the scratching continues without ceasing, the skin and flesh will inevitably be torn and damaged. Over a long period of scratching, the bone itself eventually becomes visible. This sore...

<summary>This page provides a vinegar-based remedy for scabies using puncturevine and begins a diagnostic analysis of "Bone-Envy Sores," emphasizing that their extreme itchiness signifies a deep-seated "Yin" toxin located along the bladder meridian of the back.</summary>

# Volume 8: Bone-Poison Abscesses | Page 10 | The Studio of Sixteen Elegances

Bone-Poison Abscesses <note>original: *Gudu Yongchuang*; deep, destructive infections that penetrate toward the bone.</note> arise on the inner aspect of both thighs at the Winnowing Gate point <note>original: *Jimen*; an acupuncture point (SP-11) located on the inner thigh, roughly six inches above the knee.</note>. The Winnowing Gate point on the leg originally belongs to the Foot Greater Yin Spleen Meridian <term>One of the twelve primary channels in traditional Chinese medicine; the Spleen meridian is vital for the transformation of nutrients and the "holding" of blood within the vessels.</term>. If the Spleen's energy is flourishing, then the Qi <note>The vital life force or breath.</note> and blood will flow and circulate; even if there is "fire-poison" <note>A traditional term for acute, hot inflammation or infection.</note>, the heat will inevitably dissipate with ease. Even if the heat does not dissipate and a sore forms, it will certainly be mild and easy to heal. 

Generally, a mild case will be painful, whereas a severe case will manifest as scrofulous lumps <note>original: *luo*; hard, knot-like swellings.</note>. If a sore forms that is not painful but "erupts with phlegm" <note>original: *fatan*; in this context, it refers to the formation of cold, non-red nodules or deep-seated fluid rather than respiratory phlegm.</note>, it will certainly be difficult to treat. Another name for this is "Leg Eruption" <note>original: *Tuifa*.</note>; on the twelfth day of the illness, it may be lanced. If the pus is yellow or red, it can be treated; however, if it is thin, bluish, and has a putrid odor, it is incurable. If the sore appears as a white swelling, it means the poison has transformed the bone itself; this is fundamentally a difficult condition to treat. Should the abscess rupture outwardly, and no pus appears within ten days, the patient will surely die.

# Bone-Aching Sore

Bone-Aching Sores <note>original: *Gusuanchuang*.</note> arise above the two hip bones, specifically within the area of the Jump Round point <note>original: *Huantiao* (GB-30); a major acupuncture point located at the hip joint, vital for leg movement.</note>. They begin small and then grow large; both the tendons and bones will ache. When the sore opens, it discharges fluid; if the fluid is exhausted, the patient will die. If sores and swelling appear symmetrically on both hips, not one in ten will survive. Do not say it is "just a sore" <note>original: *chuang*; usually implying a superficial lesion.</note> and not "an abscess" <note>original: *yong*; implying a deep, suppurative infection.</note>, thereby treating it lightly. When a sore arises here, it is difficult to lie on either side. Use a large Puffball <note>original: *Mapibo* (Lasiosphaera fenzlii); a fungus used in traditional medicine for its soft texture and ability to stop bleeding and clear toxins.</note> to pad the swelling so it is not rubbed. Internally, take "Supplementing the Center and Boosting Qi" medicine to treat it.

### Red Flame Sores

Red Flame Sores <note>original: *Chiyan chuang*; likely referring to an eruptive rash or scarlet-colored lesions.</note> appear as red spots across the entire body. These are produced when the Lung Meridian of Hand-Taiyin <note>one of the twelve primary pathways of Qi, associated with the respiratory system and the skin's surface.</note> is affected by Wind-Heat <note>a "pathogenic factor" in Chinese medicine similar to an infectious or inflammatory onset characterized by fever and rashes.</note>.

<summary>This page provides a grim prognosis for "hollow-hard" red sores but offers hope through a specific herbal decoction for arm ulcers, followed by a diagnosis of "Red Flame Sores" as a lung-meridian disorder.</summary>
<keywords>Modified Ginseng and Astragalus Decoction, Old Liver Sores, Red Flame Sores, Lung Meridian of Hand-Taiyin, Wind-Heat, Chen Shiduo, Traditional Chinese Medicine</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the discussion of "Red Flame Sores" mentioned on the previous page, detailing the underlying pathology of the Lungs and Blood before providing a specific herbal formula transmitted by the Daoist immortal Wu Peng.</meta>

Hall of Great Elegance <note>original: *Daya Tang*; likely the name of the author's studio or the publishing house.</note>

The Lungs govern the skin and body hair. When the Lung meridian <note>The "meridians" are pathways through which vital energy (Qi) flows; the Lung meridian is specifically associated with respiratory and skin health.</note> has an excess of Qi but an insufficiency of Blood, "Wind-Heat" <note>A traditional diagnostic category referring to external pathogens that cause fever, inflammation, and dryness.</note> becomes trapped within the Lungs and is difficult to discharge. Without enough Blood to moisten the area, this heat lingers in the skin and hair rather than dispersing. This condition is also known as "Red Flame Wind." Because of the heat in the Lungs, "Heart Fire" also invades; this creates a situation where "fire assists fire," and the body’s Blood is further consumed. As the Blood is depleted, the Lung Qi becomes even hotter. This is why red spots appear more prominently. These spots may come and go, but if the condition persists for a long time without healing, there are cases where it transforms into "Wind-Leprosy" <term>original: *laifeng*; a historical term for severe, chronic, or disfiguring skin diseases, including leprosy.</term>. Therefore, the method of treatment must focus on dissipating the "Wind" and reducing the heat; once this is done, the sores will heal on their own.

### Blood Scrofula
<note>original: *Xueqi Luo*; scrofula typically refers to chronic swellings or nodules, often appearing in the neck or armpit areas, historically associated with glandular issues.</note>

Blood and Qi scrofula grows between the ribs and the armpits <note>specifically the *Yuanye* point, located on the side of the chest.</note>. This area is governed by the Foot Lesser Yang Gallbladder Meridian <note>In traditional Chinese medicine, meridians are channels through which life force, or Qi, flows. The Gallbladder meridian runs along the side of the body.</note>. The Gallbladder belongs to the "Wood" element. If the Wood Qi were flowing freely and smoothly, how could these nodules ever form? When the Gallbladder’s Wood energy is suppressed and does not flow freely, the "Wood" cannot properly discharge or circulate, which leads to the frequent formation of these scrofulous nodules. 

In principle, women suffer from more "stagnation" <term>original: *Yu*; a core concept in Chinese medicine referring to the blockage of emotional and physical energy, often caused by repressed feelings.</term> while men suffer less. A man’s stagnation is easily resolved, but a woman’s stagnation is difficult to unlock. Therefore, in men, these nodules are easily dispersed, but in women, they are difficult to cure. It often happens that these nodules form into hard lumps that do not change for years, only to suddenly swell, protrude, rupture, and leak black fluid, leading to death. 

For this reason, when treating this type of scrofula, it is essential that the patient first release all worries and sorrows <note>The author emphasizes that emotional release is a prerequisite for the medicine to work.</note>; only then will taking medicinal decoctions be effective. Although these nodules form within the Gallbladder...

<summary>This page concludes a treatment for Lung Heat and introduces "Blood Scrofula," attributing its cause to emotional stagnation that blocks the Gallbladder meridian, particularly in women.</summary>
<keywords>Blood Scrofula, Gallbladder Meridian, Wood Element, Emotional Stagnation, Pulse Diagnosis, Women's Health, Chen Shiduo</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the discussion on Blood Scrofula, focusing on the relationship between the Liver and Gallbladder meridians and the danger of using improper "detoxifying" treatments that can lead to more serious abscesses.</meta>

When the meridian is stagnant, if the Gallbladder is stagnant, then the Liver is also stagnant. When the Liver and Gallbladder are stagnant together, they both become diseased. The Qi <note>The vital life force or energy that flows through the body's meridians.</note> of the Liver most ideally flows freely and smoothly; it should never be blocked. If the Liver Qi is blocked and suppressed, the Liver Blood will inevitably surge upward, causing swelling, protrusions, and eventual collapse. This is not the result of Qi flowing freely, but rather the destruction and breakdown of the blood. 

Therefore, in treating this type of sore, one must first use medicinal herbs that soothe and soften the Liver <note>In traditional Chinese medicine, "softening the Liver" often involves nourishing the blood to calm the organ's "wind" or "fire" nature.</note>, while assisting them with substances that generate Blood and Qi. In this way, the Liver and Gallbladder will be brought into harmony, the stagnation will naturally dissipate, and the sore will be healed. 

If a practitioner does not understand this method of treatment and recklessly employs "toxin-defeating" agents <note>original: *baidu*; typically very cold, harsh herbs used to clear heat, which can sometimes further damage the body's healthy energy if used incorrectly</note>, the sore will remain hidden within the flesh, causing a dull, lingering pain. Alternatively, it may suddenly enlarge and spread toward the chest, manifesting at the *Qimen* point <note>original: *Qimen*; the "Gate of Hope," an acupuncture point located on the ribcage, serving as the terminal point of the Liver meridian.</note> and becoming an axillary abscess <note>An abscess in the armpit area.</note>. One must be extremely cautious!

### Heavenly Pock Sores

Heavenly Pock Sores <note>original: *Tianpaochuang*; likely referring to a blistering skin condition such as bullous impetigo or pemphigus, characterized by large, water-filled blisters.</note> appear on the head, face, and across the entire body, including the hands and feet. This occurs because toxins have become knotted within the skin and hair <note>In traditional Chinese medicine, the "skin and hair" represent the outermost protective layer of the body, governed by the Lungs.</note>, but have not yet penetrated into the *Nutritive* or *Defensive* levels <note>original: *Ying* and *Wei*; these are internal layers of the body's energy system. Diseases remaining in the outer skin are generally considered less life-threatening than those that reach these deeper levels.</note>.

In principle, this condition is considered relatively mild. However, if the method of treatment is incorrect, the pain becomes unbearable—no different from being branded by hot iron or burned by fire. This sore is caused by a deficiency in <term>Lung Qi</term> <note>The vital energy of the Lungs, which is responsible for maintaining the health and "closing" of the skin's pores.</note>, which allows fire-toxins to become knotted within the Lungs. This is originally rooted in the steaming effects of summer heat and dampness...

# Esoteric Meanings of the Grotto Heavens: Volume 8

->Hall of Great Elegance<-

The disease of **Scrofula** <note>original: *Luoli*; a term for chronic swelling or nodules of the lymph nodes, typically in the neck. In modern medicine, this is often associated with tuberculous cervical lymphadenitis.</note> has many names and takes many forms. Generally, there are nine ways this ailment is contracted:

1. Due to **anger**.
2. Due to **depression** <note>original: *Yu*; referring to emotional stagnation or bottled-up feelings that disrupt the flow of vital energy.</note>.
3. Due to eating food that has been nibbled on by **rats**.
4. Due to eating food damaged by **mole crickets, scorpions, or caterpillars**.
5. Due to eating **honey-based** things <note>This may refer to contaminated honey or a specific dietary sensitivity believed to trigger the condition.</note>.
6. Due to eating food that **centipedes** have crawled over.
7. Due to **excessive joy** while overindulging in fruit.
8. Due to **indulging in lust**, which damages the **Kidneys**, combined with overeating "bloody" foods <note>This likely refers to rich, heavy meats or raw foods that were believed to create "damp-heat" in the body.</note>.
9. Due to **fright and terror**, or sleeping in a poor position <note>Literally "losing the pillow," resulting in a stiff neck or "wry neck," which the author links to the obstruction of Qi.</note> so that the vital energy (*Qi*) does not flow smoothly.

When these sores first emerge, they often appear between the neck and the armpits, or they may spread across the chest and the sides of the ribs. In shape and size, they resemble a plum pit; they may be mobile or static, long or round, and may appear in connected chains or as isolated lumps.

When they reach the stage of ulceration and rot, the discharge varies, consisting of fluid, pus, or blood. Before the sores have broken, they are easy to medicalize and treat. However, after they have ruptured, it is difficult to achieve a successful cure. This is because before they break, although the body may be in a state of deficiency, it has not yet reached the point of damaging the **Five Internal Organs** <note>original: *Wuzang*; the Heart, Liver, Spleen, Lungs, and Kidneys, which are the fundamental functional systems in traditional Chinese medicine.</note>. Once they have ulcerated, the body's vitality is gradually depleted, making it difficult to remedy the damage caused by the **Seven Emotions** <note>original: *Qiqing*; joy, anger, worry, pensiveness, sadness, fear, and shock. Traditional medicine teaches that extreme emotions directly injure the internal organs.</note>. Therefore, one must...

<summary>This page lists the nine causes of scrofula, ranging from emotional distress and lifestyle choices to environmental toxins, and describes the physical progression of the disease from nodules to ulceration.</summary>
<keywords>scrofula, Luoli, nine causes, Seven Emotions, Five Organs, neck sores, lymphadenitis, Chen Shiduo</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the discussion on scrofula, moving from the nine causes mentioned on the previous page to the core diagnostic principles and the three primary strategies for treatment based on pulse diagnosis.</meta>

To reduce what is excessive and supplement what is deficient <term>original: *Sun Bu*; the foundational TCM principle of "reducing" excess energy or "supplementing" a lack of energy to restore balance</term>, thereby rescuing the body from its injuries, is the beginning of a truly subtle and effective method. 

However, although there are nine causes for this disease, there are only three primary methods for treating it:
1. Treating the **Liver and Gallbladder**.
2. Treating the **Spleen and Stomach**.
3. Treating the **Heart and Kidneys**.

For those whose treatment lies in the **Liver and Gallbladder**, the pulse at the left **guan** position <note>The "guan" (gate) position is one of the three spots on the wrist used in pulse diagnosis. The left side corresponds to the Liver and Gallbladder.</note> will inevitably feel **astringent** <note>original: *Se*; a "choppy" pulse often compared to a knife scraping bamboo, indicating a lack of fluid or the stagnation of vital energy (*Qi*).</note>, while the pulse at the right **guan** position <note>The right "guan" position corresponds to the Spleen and Stomach.</note> will feel **slippery** <note>original: *Hua*; a smooth, rounded pulse often compared to beads rolling in a basin, typically indicating the presence of Phlegm or Dampness.</note>. 

This occurs because stagnation within the Liver and Gallbladder, if left unaddressed, will inevitably descend and "attack" the **Earth** of the Spleen and Stomach <note>In the Five Elements theory, Wood (Liver) controls Earth (Spleen). When Wood is stressed or stagnant, it overpowers and weakens Earth, disrupting digestion.</note>. When the **Earth Qi** is suppressed, it becomes difficult for the body to transform water and grain into nourishment. This leads to the production of **Phlegm**, which encourages the formation of "knots" or nodules; thus, the scrofula refuses to resolve. By treating the Liver and Gallbladder to dissolve this stagnant phlegm and fluid, the scrofula will easily be transformed and cleared.

For those whose treatment lies in the **Spleen and Stomach**, the pulse at the right **guan** position will be **floating and weak**, or perhaps **slippery and forceful**. This clearly indicates that within the Spleen and Stomach, there is nothing but the rising of **Phlegm-Qi** and the desolation of **Earth Qi**. 

If one does not strengthen the Spleen, the phlegm cannot be eliminated; if one does not strengthen the Stomach, the fluids cannot be transformed. As these fluids and phlegm increase daily, the nodules of the scrofula become hardened and difficult to break apart. How can the disease be cured under such conditions? Therefore, one must **greatly supplement** the Spleen and Stomach to dissolve the phlegm and fluids, and then assist this process with detoxifying...

<summary>This page outlines the three diagnostic paths for treating scrofula, focusing on how imbalances in the Liver, Spleen, and Stomach lead to the accumulation of phlegm and the formation of nodules.</summary>
<keywords>pulse diagnosis, guan position, Five Elements, Liver and Gallbladder, Spleen and Stomach, Phlegm (Tan), Earth Qi, scrofula treatment</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: This page concludes the diagnostic overview of the three treatment methods for scrofula (scrofulous neck lumps) by detailing the Heart-Kidney pulse patterns and offering a specific herbal formula for Liver-Gallbladder stagnation.</meta>

...with these medicinal flavors, the disease will be swept away as if by a broom. For those whose treatment lies in the **Heart and Kidneys**, when palpating the **left cun** <note>The "inch" position on the left wrist, which corresponds to the Heart in traditional pulse diagnosis.</note> pulse, it will inevitably feel **slippery**. Conversely, the **right chi** <note>The "cubit" or "foot" position on the right wrist, which corresponds to the Kidney and the Gate of Life (*Mingmen*).</note> pulse will feel **astringent**. 

This clearly indicates that the Heart and Kidneys are divided and cannot achieve **mutual completion** <term>original: *Ji Ji*; a state where the Fire of the Heart descends to warm the Kidneys, and the Water of the Kidneys ascends to cool the Heart, maintaining balance</term>. When these two systems fail to harmonize, the Liver, Gallbladder, Spleen, and Stomach likewise fail to respond to one another. Consequently, **phlegm nodules** <term>original: *Tan He*; firm lumps caused by the accumulation of stagnant fluids</term> do not dissipate, and the **scrofulous chains** <note>referring to the characteristic "string" of lumps associated with scrofula</note> become even more severe. 

By supplementing the Heart and Kidneys, the **Yin and Yang** will reach a state of harmony. If one then adds a small amount of assistant herbs that **remove toxins and break through hardness** <note>refers to herbs specifically chosen to soften firm masses and clear infection</note>, the benefits will be achieved with even greater speed. 

If a practitioner does not understand these three methods of treatment and recklessly employs the **knife and needle** <note>referring to invasive surgical lancing or intensive acupuncture</note>, they will only further deplete the patient’s **fundamental vitality** <term>original: *Gen Ben*; the core "root" energy of the body</term>. How could such a course of action ever remedy the situation? It will inevitably lead the patient to become "neighbors with death." Is this not a profound and heavy pity?

# Cinnabar for Cultivating Earth and Transforming Toxins

## The Camphor Pellet

According to *The Essentials of Vital Methods* <note>original: *Huofa Jiyao*; a reference to a classic medical text, likely that of the Yuan dynasty physician Zhu Danxi</note>, this formula treats scrofula <term>scrofula: chronic swelling of the lymph nodes, often associated with tuberculosis</term> that has become ulcerated and rotten, spreading to the chest and both armpits with lumps as large as eggplants. It also treats cases that have spread to the shoulders and have remained unhealed for four or five years. Its efficacy is miraculous.

Camphor <gloss>3 mace</gloss> <note>A mace (qian) is a traditional unit of weight approximately equal to 3.75 grams.</note>

Wash again three or four times. Afterward, mix the Camphor and Realgar powder with **sesame oil** and brush it onto the wounds. Fluid will be discharged. On the following day, wash and apply the mixture again. Continue this process until the condition is healed. One must strictly avoid alcohol and sexual activity.

# Inner and Outer Leg Ulcers

Leg ulcers <term>leg ulcers: known in Chinese as *lianchuang*, these are chronic, difficult-to-heal sores on the lower legs, often corresponding to varicose ulcers or ecthyma</term> are distinguished by whether they are "inner" or "outer." The **inner leg ulcer** pertains to the region of the **Foot Jueyin Liver Meridian**, while the **outer leg ulcer** pertains to the region of the **Foot Yangming Stomach Meridian**. It might seem that the outer ulcer is milder than the inner one, as the Stomach is a "bowel" <note>original: *fu*; in Chinese medicine, the "six bowels" are hollow organs responsible for digestion and transmission</note> characterized by an abundance of both **Qi** <term>Qi: the vital energy or life force that circulates through the body</term> and **Blood**, whereas the Liver is an organ with an abundance of Blood but a deficiency of Qi. 

However, it is difficult to truly differentiate leg ulcers by "inner" or "outer" causes; if the internal organs did not harbor **damp-toxin** <term>damp-toxin: a pathological combination of environmental humidity and internal heat/inflammation that "sinks" to the lower body</term>, then these sores would not arise on the left, right, inner, or outer sides at all. While most leg ulcers are caused by the body contracting external dampness which then forms a sore, there are also cases caused by physical trauma—strikes, falls, scratching, or bumps—or by being bitten and wounded by **poisonous insects or vicious dogs**. 

If the Stomach did not already possess inherent damp-toxin, these injuries would not necessarily remain unhealed for such a long time. Therefore, the method of treatment is to **invigorate the blood** to remove the dampness; if this is done, the bone will not necessarily decay. Unfortunately, people today do not observe the proper prohibitions. They overindulge in **sexual activity** <note>original: *fangshi*; traditional Chinese medicine often warns that excessive sexual activity depletes the "Kidney Essence," weakening the body's ability to heal chronic sores</note>, leading to the skin turning black and the flesh rotting until the foul stench becomes unbearable. Furthermore, in the case of women, if during their menstrual period the blood...

<summary>This page introduces the classification of leg ulcers based on the Liver and Stomach meridians, arguing that while they appear external, their root cause is internal "damp-toxin" exacerbated by lifestyle choices.</summary>

Internally, one should use a formula that supplements the center and resolves toxins; externally, apply the "Divine Paper-Separated Plaster." With only a few applications, the wound will heal rapidly.

### Human-Face Sores

**Human-face sores** appear either upon the knees <note>original: *niuxi*. While this is also the name of a herb, here it refers to the anatomical location of the knee joint.</note> or upon the elbows. The shape of the sore remarkably resembles a human face. In severe cases, it possesses a mouth, a nose, and eyes. These are most often caused by **ghosts or spirits** <note>original: *guiwu*. In the 16th century, certain intractable or "uncanny" diseases were attributed to external spiritual influences or karmic debt.</note> possessing the body. 

However, even though the mouth, nose, and eyes are present, the sore usually cannot speak, and in most cases, it cannot move. When it does move, its appearance is one of profound distress and suffering. If one places meat or food into the "mouth" of the sore, it is truly able to digest it. The ancients' claims that these sores can eat are not a falsehood. 

There is another variety where the mouth and eyes are immobile; these seem not to be the result of ghostly possession.

<summary>This page provides the final preparation instructions for a medicinal plaster and describes the uncanny pathology of Human-Face Sores, including their appearance and the belief that they can consume food.</summary>

<keywords>Human-Face Sores, Renmian chuang, TCM pharmacology, Verdigris, Dragon Bone, spiritual possession, karmic illness, external medicine</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: This page concludes the theoretical discussion on the origins of Human-Face Sores—specifically their classification into "living" and "dead" varieties—and provides the "Light Thunder Pill" recipe for treatment, emphasizing that physical medicine must be accompanied by spiritual repentance.</meta>

Something possesses it. Yet, if it is not a ghostly entity, why would a mouth and eyes grow within a sore? One does not realize that **human-face sores** are originally categorized into two types: "Living" and "Dead." The **living** ones are capable of eating and moving; the **dead** ones can neither move nor consume food. In truth, both types involve a haunting or curse. It is either a punishment from Heaven or a <term>karmic debt: *yuannie*, a manifestation of retribution for past wrongs</term> arriving of its own accord. One must engage in self-reflection, fasting, and ritual offerings of thanks before applying medicine; only then can a cure be achieved.

### Light Thunder Pill
<note>original: *Qing Lei Wan*. The name likely derives from the lead ingredient, *Leiwan* (Thunder Pill), and *Qingfen* (Calomel).</note>

Transmitted by the **Celestial Master Qi Bo** <note>Qi Bo is a legendary mythological figure and physician, often cited as the teacher of the Yellow Emperor in foundational Chinese medical texts.</note> for the divinely effective treatment of both living and dead human-face sores.

| Ingredient | Dosage |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Omphalia** | <gloss>three qian</gloss> <note>original: *leiwan*. A medicinal fungus historically used to expel parasites and "drive out evil."</note> |
| **Calomel** | <gloss>one qian</gloss> <note>original: *qingfen*. Mercurous chloride, a potent but toxic mineral traditionally used for stubborn skin diseases and deep-seated "toxins."</note> |
| **Poria** | <gloss>one qian</gloss> <note>original: *baifuling*. A fungus that grows on pine roots, used here to drain "dampness" or excess fluids.</note> |

Grind each into an extremely fine powder and mix them evenly. Apply the powder topically, and the sore will immediately dissipate. In principle, **Omphalia** is most capable of removing toxins and expelling evil influences. **Calomel** penetrates deep into the bone marrow; where could the evil hide then? **Poria** simply serves to remove the "watery dampness" <note>In traditional medicine, "dampness" is a pathological factor that causes swelling and discharge.</note> from the area. Together, these ingredients achieve a miraculous success. If one adds repentance and prayer to the regimen, it will provide even greater blessings for one's future well-being.

# Blood-Wind Sores
<note>original: *Xuefengchuang*. In traditional Chinese medicine, "Wind" often refers to conditions characterized by intense itching or symptoms that move across the body, while "Blood" refers to the underlying vital fluid being affected.</note>

Blood-wind sores mostly occur on both the inner and outer sides of the legs, extending from above the knees down to the front of the ankle bones. Most people claim these arise because the blood has been attacked by <term>wind-evil: *fengxie*, an external pathogenic force in Chinese medicine associated with itching and sudden onset</term>. Little do they realize that the sufferers are almost always heavy drinkers who consume excessive alcohol. This leads to <term>dampness: *shi*, a heavy, lingering pathogen</term> stagnating in the lower legs rather than dispersing. Once the <term>qi: vital energy</term> and blood decline, the sores gradually manifest.

When these sores first emerge, they are invariably small and itchy. Over time, the itching becomes so intense that it cannot be stopped without vigorous scratching. However, excessive scratching inevitably damages the skin and flesh. If the patient continues to drink alcohol without restraint as before, the itching intensifies further. 

Broken skin is difficult to close, and "alcoholic dampness" is difficult to disperse; consequently, the cycle of rotting skin and putrefying flesh never ends. After a long time, the dampness trapped within the flesh will inevitably give birth to <term>lice and worms: *shichong*, refers here to parasites or larvae believed to be spontaneously generated by stagnant fluids</term>. As their numbers grow, the itching becomes even more unbearable.

The method of treatment must begin with a total <term>abstinence from alcohol: *duanjiu*</term>. Only then should one use internal medicine to supplement the qi and blood while simultaneously dispelling the wind and dampness. Externally, <term>medicinal plasters: *gaoyao*, thick herbal pastes applied to the skin</term> should be applied. Once the excess fluids are drained and the parasites are killed, the condition will heal of its own accord.

<summary>This page describes the causes and symptoms of "Blood-Wind Sores" (likely a form of chronic eczema or stasis dermatitis), attributing the condition to the stagnation of alcoholic dampness in the lower legs rather than just environmental "wind." It emphasizes that lifestyle changes, specifically quitting alcohol, are mandatory for the internal and external treatments to be effective.</summary>

<keywords>Blood-Wind Sores, wind-evil, dampness, alcoholism, parasitic infection, Qi and Blood, medicinal plasters, dermatology</keywords>
