The following recipes address both the internal systemic imbalance and the external skin damage.</meta>

# Supplementing Qi and Dispersing Dampness Decoction
Transmitted by the <term>Perfected Lord Wu Peng: a legendary divine healer and shaman from Chinese antiquity</term> for the treatment of blood-wind sores.

| | | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **White Atractylodes** <gloss>five qian (approx. 15g)</gloss> | **Poria** <gloss>three qian (approx. 9g)</gloss> | **Angelica Sinensis** <gloss>five qian</gloss> | **Astragalus** <gloss>one liang (approx. 30g)</gloss> |
| **Oak Twigs** <gloss>five qian</gloss> | **Job's Tears** <gloss>five qian</gloss> | **Raw Licorice Root** <gloss>two qian</gloss> | **Fishpoison Yam** <gloss>two qian</gloss> |
| **Cinnamon Bark** <gloss>one qian</gloss> | **Safflower** <gloss>one qian</gloss> | **Water Plantain** <gloss>two qian</gloss> | |

Decoct in water and consume. Taking many doses is ideal. 

<note>original: *Buqi Fenshi Tang*. This formula focuses on strengthening the patient's "Qi" (vital energy) with Astragalus and Atractylodes to help the body expel the "dampness" caused by alcohol consumption. Ingredients like Safflower and Cinnamon help circulate the blood to prevent the stagnation that leads to rot.</note>

For external use, apply the **Ten Spirits Ointment**.

---

# Ten Spirits Ointment
Treats blood-wind sores.

| | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Earthworm Casts** <gloss>one liang</gloss> | **Dragon’s Blood Resin** <gloss>three qian</gloss> | **Purslane** <gloss>one liang</gloss> |
| **Phellodendron Bark** <gloss>five qian</gloss> | **Calomel** <gloss>one qian</gloss> | **Tallow Tree Root** <gloss>three qian</gloss> |
| **Vermillion** <gloss>four qian</gloss> | **Lead Powder** <gloss>three qian</gloss> | **Camphor** <gloss>two qian</gloss> |
| **Musk** <gloss>three fen (approx. 0.9g)</gloss> | | |

Grind each into a fine powder, mix with pig lard to form an ointment, and apply to the affected area.

<note>original: *Shishen Gao*. Many of these ingredients, such as Dragon's Blood (a resin) and Purslane, are used to stop bleeding and reduce inflammation. Note that historical ingredients like Calomel (mercurous chloride), Vermillion (mercuric sulfide), and Lead Powder are highly toxic by modern medical standards, though they were common in 16th-century topical treatments for severe skin infections.</note>

<summary>This page outlines two primary treatments for Blood-Wind Sores: an internal herbal decoction to strengthen the body and drain dampness, and an external medicated plaster to heal the skin.</summary>
<keywords>Blood-Wind Sores, Supplementing Qi and Dispersing Dampness Decoction, Ten Spirits Ointment, Wu Peng, traditional Chinese pharmacology, alcoholic dampness, topical remedies</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page concludes the instructions for the "Ten Spirits Ointment" introduced on the previous page as a treatment for blood-wind sores. It details the application method, the cleaning of the wound between applications, and the physician's strict lifestyle requirements for the patient.</meta>

->Esoteric Meanings of the Grotto Heavens: Volume 8<-
->Page 34<-
->Great Elegance Hall<-

Apply the ointment to a piece of oiled paper cut to the size of the sore and place it over the wound. Wrap the area with a cloth and bind it firmly. Allow the fluid <note>This refers to the "dampness" or pus being drawn out of the skin.</note> to drain out. After using several of these plasters in succession, the fluid will eventually dry up. When changing the medicated plaster, use one <gloss>liang (approx. 37.5g)</gloss> of **Honeysuckle** <note>original: *Jinyinhua*. This herb is traditionally used to clear heat and resolve toxins.</note> decocted into a warm wash for the wound. Then, apply a fresh plaster. If there is no more fluid flowing out, there is no need to change the plaster frequently. Applying a few more will surely achieve success.

However, if the patient does not sever sexual desire and abstain from alcohol, there is no need for me to treat them. <note>Chen Shiduo frequently emphasizes that medicine alone cannot overcome a patient's poor lifestyle choices, particularly "excessive desires" and alcohol, which he believed generated the internal heat and dampness causing these sores.</note>

### End of Volume 8 of Esoteric Meanings of the Grotto Heavens
